this weekend it became known that mr van rompuy has promised 5 billion in aid to egypt and i have serious questions about that aid commitment. in recent months egypt has moved further away from a democratic state of law rather than closer to it. the position of coptic christians has deteriorated, relations with israel are under pressure and the entire supreme court has been sidelined in the drafting of the new constitution. i would therefore like to know, mr president, what conditions are attached to this aid, but i also have questions about the budgetary coverage of this aid, plus the question of why van rompuy made this announcement and not mrs ashton. i would therefore like to hear an explanation from mr van rompuy and a debate in the near future. and i would like to ask you to take that question with you: when can we debate this, possibly as early as february? that's my question to you: when can we debate this with mr van rompuy?
the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the community's financial resources are used to the full. it is necessary to respond to changes in trade, in demand, in production methods. it is also necessary for a transition to a green economy. but it can be bad if companies do not provide adequate care for workers affected by reorganization. especially in these times of crisis, it is a major shortcoming that the eu does not have a minimum standard, actual minimum rights for the care of workers. the practice in the member states is now very different, as is sometimes painfully evident in applications to the globalisation adjustment fund. employees seem to be disposable items in some cases. the eu wants businesses to reorganise to become more innovative, sustainable and competitive. but if we want that, we need the support of citizens and workers. we need to ensure those basic rights to enable people to deal with a little confidence in a changing economy. only then will we get that support. i am rather disappointed that employers and some member states are opposed to legislation in this area. i would think it is in everyone's interest to have a level playing field and to have well-prepared, flexible employees with confidence in the future. the ball is in your court now, commissioner andor.
this plenary debate, with four reports, is a fine extension of what we have already established in the mandate we are now negotiating with the member states, with the support of the european commission. and it really shows in the report on energy policy, in the report on spatial planning and in the report on urban development that we are pursuing an investment policy together, setting up investment funds and that we are going to do things differently. the same applies to the european solidarity fund, which helps in the event of disasters. this new approach - as mrs krehl has already said - in the form of a national contract, a partnership contract, allows member states to make choices in terms of knowledge and innovation, a smarter and greener europe, competitiveness and, above all, people at work. i would also like to mention in this new approach the smart specialisation in which we are connecting top people at different levels and giving a major boost to european integration. we are therefore moving in my view in europe at a higher rate of speed. it should and it can. too much of it will be doubled, and we can do a lot better. i think, ladies and gentlemen, that we should take a look at this point in the debate and see whether we are making it sufficiently visible with the member states. i note from my contacts in the member states that there is a lot of work going on, but that there is very little real communication with the people involved, with the companies, the universities and other partners in the regions and cities. if we go back to that citizen in 2014 and say, the biggest budget is the investment budget of the cohesion funds, so we have to give it more colour and flair. i call on the commissioner to come up with a real communication plan, before this year's holiday or just after, but with more than just a plan. five funds are asking for an extra commitment and i'm still seeing way too little of it.

in writing. − the report does not demand any substantial obligations on companies and employers as regards restructuring processes, nor does it seek to improve workers’ rights to information and consultation. it considers workers to be a flexibility reserve who have to ‘adapt’ to any changing circumstances or new strategy of the company, companies should strive to increase their ‘competitiveness’ by becoming ‘flexible firms’, also by promoting workers’ ‘internal and external mobility’. despite these shortcomings i voted in favour of the report because it puts pressure on the commission to take legislative action on information and consultation of workers, at which point these shortcomings can be addressed.
everyone was happy this morning, because the netherlands remains a triple-a country, according to standard & poor's, one of the rating agencies. but the netherlands has a record low consumer confidence, a negative individual savings rate, skyrocketing unemployment, enormously increased tax burdens and a government that is not cutting anything on senseless spending. and then the hundreds of billions in guarantees and loans to greece, portugal, spanish banks and the esm must also be written off, because that money will never come back. and all that means is a triple a, the highest score! and there's no word of criticism on this. there was criticism when the rating agencies called greece bankrupt. brussels did not allow this and this parliament now wants to set up its own rating agency which looks positively at europe. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. the real state of the union is not what we want to hear here.
mr president, when the euro was introduced, all the countries had identical interest rates, and that has proved fatal. countries that traditionally rely on high inflation to evaporate excessive government debt continued to spend like crazy. that's why they're paying higher interest rates now. that's how the market works. but socialists and euro-lovers reject this market operation and that is why we need to have euro bonds. the member states are then jointly responsible for all the public debts and thus countries that have borrowed themselves into the grave are wrongly charged interest on the basis of the creditworthiness of countries that have taken slightly better care. rewarding bad behaviour and punishing good behaviour is the real core value of the european union. the result is that even more money will be spent that is not there so that we can explain to our grandchildren that their tax money has already been spent on the failed experiment of the sacred euro. is it technically possible to introduce eurobonds? yes, of course, i'm sure. do we have to do it? no, of course not!
the european parliament is in favour of the gradual pooling of debts in the euro area. but it is not enlargement that is needed, but the reduction of debt. eurobonds facilitate lending for euro area countries whose public finances and economic structure are in a weak condition. normally, financial markets stimulate these countries to cut back and reform, so that their sovereign bonds are again a safe investment. eurobonds weaken these incentives. this eliminates the risks of unequal economies. we have seen what this has led to since the introduction of the euro. the weak euro countries are now implementing reforms that should have been introduced earlier. the result: the commission has not yet received any comments from the applicant. euro bonds, on the other hand, are mainly a panacea and do not address the underlying causes of the macroeconomic imbalances in the euro area.
i would like to thank the rapporteur, mr pallone, for the good cooperation he has shown in this report on public finances, and i would also like to thank all the shadow rapporteurs for this report, which gives a balanced picture of where we are with public finances. i think we can all agree that we have to look very, very closely at these public finances. we must look at longterm reform and we certainly do not want investment in non-productive sectors or non-productive growth. that's very clear. but what the report also says very well is that we have problems in the eurozone. the economy will shrink in 2013, and that's partly because of that agenda of very strict austerity policies that have been implemented in recent years. that is also made clear in the report. we must not only focus on those cuts, but we must also look at investments to ensure that the economy in europe grows again. not the non-productive sectors, but simply the sectors that are going to make sure that we are going to generate sustainable growth again. that's important too. i have a question to the commission, to commissioner olli rehn, who says: we are already taking a great deal of this into account and this is mainly a continuation of what the commission has been doing in the semester. i often read other things in the commission's recommendations. i read this report and it is quite clear that parliament is calling for a shift in taxation away from labour towards environmental costs. how often do we see very clear recommendations from the commission making it very clear that in all the countries of the eu those taxes really need to be shifted? how often do we read in the recommendations that tax evasion should be tackled? how often do we read that it is really time for investment? how often do we read in recommendations that unnecessary defence spending should be reduced? it can be much tighter and this report really shows that the commission is only halfway there. yes, long-term investment is needed, but it is particularly important that the commission now tackles real issues such as tax evasion, more tax shifts away from labour to environmental costs, and away from unnecessary expenditure such as defence. the commission should make a much stronger effort to do so in the coming six months, and i would like to hear the commissioner say the same.

the fisheries policy must be environmentally, economically and socially sustainable. we're going to make some important decisions tomorrow. how are we gonna do that? rational or emotional? the debate on the discard ban has been very black and white. the reality is more complex. care is important. i've asked norway for advice on the disposal. they started reducing waste 25 years ago. some important lessons are: one, it can only be done step by step. the norwegians have started with a discard ban for only one species. two, it's learning by doing. the third. the norwegians say: 100% reduction is not possible. tailor-made measures and exceptions are needed, particularly in mixed fisheries. the epp amendment to article 15 is therefore very good. this amendment is in keeping with the complex reality. tailor-made work is only possible through the multiannual plans. i support that amendment. care is important. we are not here to legislate! let us vote tomorrow on legislation that is both ambitious and feasible.
the north sea has always been one of the most important fishing grounds in europe. this sea, the north sea, seemed bottomless, a kind of perpetual motion. it couldn't open, the fish kept coming. and then it was 1977. the last herring looked like it was fished out of the north sea. fishermen desperately switched to other, not yet commercial, fish species. europe was faced with a six-year ban on herring fishing. and let me repeat that: for six years, no herring was caught! such a measure would be politically impossible today, just look at the bluefin tuna. but after six years the situation was restored and the ship was able to sail again. 45 years later, we realize that the world's oceans are what the north sea was then, no longer bottomless, but one big aquarium. commissioner and mrs rodust, i would like to thank you very much for your efforts. and, ladies and gentlemen, whether we like it or not, whether it suits us politically or not, the laws of nature are ultimately the ones that determine the limits and not the short-term economic interests.
mr president, the fisheries sector is indeed about sustainability and at the same time about regionalisation, innovation and modernisation. and there's a good suggestion. i am referring to the dynamics in the sector. such a transferable, negotiable quota instrument is a good instrument for this. it also helps to take old boats out of service and at the same time introduce more efficient and better fishing boats. the member states are best placed to ensure that this is done. innovation is also a sector-wide issue. look at the by-catch, too. there are new systems that allow you to put the fish overboard alive, without killing it. there are systems that make that possible. we should encourage this kind of innovation, not block it. i would ask the commissioner to look at the example of dutch fishermen from volendam, the schilder brothers, who have developed such a fish-pulling machine. finally, the european funds, not only the fisheries fund but also the regional funds, make it possible to work in a different way. the aim is to stimulate economic and social activities in coastal areas. i wanted to emphasize this, especially for the coming years. it is not just about sustainability at sea, but also in coastal zones for the people who work in the fisheries.
mr president, my support today will depend on mrs fraga's amendment. it is a crucial amendment which must be adopted, because a complete ban on discarding will lead to major problems for the fisheries sector. it is therefore important that the commission first look at the effects of such a discard ban. because to introduce such a measure all at once will have a very bad effect, especially on the mixed fisheries sector. and after that, let us determine what concrete measures are feasible, especially for the control plans by species. after such an examination we can gradually reduce unwanted by-catches. there is one major contradiction in this whole debate, and that is the ban on discarding. europe wants to introduce a ban on discarding, but why is it that many do not want to introduce the pulse corks? the pulse is an innovative fishing technique that greatly reduces by-catches. we have to allow the pulsar!

on behalf of the s&d group, as it turns out, every vote counts. and so it will be tomorrow. this is also the case throughout the european union, where noise is one of the major health problems. over 50,000 heart attacks can be directly related to noise pollution. this is especially true for low-income people who live near highways, in crowded cities, usually in the most poorly insulated homes. they are the ones who are most exposed to noise. then the question is every time how do you balance that against employment? i'll get back to that. how is the noise problem being solved at the moment? by doing some symptom management. very high noise barriers are being erected all over europe, sometimes even with the help of the structural funds. in some countries we have seen a huge race in recent years to build huge, high sound barriers behind which there are cows. it could be much simpler, namely tackling the noise at the source. and the source of the sound, that's the car! it is also the car engines that can be changed at a fairly low cost, up to 60 euros per engine for passenger cars and 750 euros per truck. we in the european union always talk about job creation and innovation. then we must show that we mean it. we are talking about possible impacts on employment, but is it not true, commissioner, that your own impact assessment makes it very clear that you do not expect any job losses here, but rather job creation? if implemented properly, this scheme will bring innovation, lower health costs and, most importantly, better health for all europeans.

we are actually having another incredibly classic debate. we identify a theme, noise pollution, that a lot of people are suffering from. we want to do something about that. and we also know that europe must do that because there has to be a level playing field for all car manufacturers. but then, when we want to do something about it, the conservative side immediately calls out: this is too expensive for the manufacturers, they can't have this now. it's always a crisis. so all of a sudden we have to take it easy. and suddenly we're not talking about the cost that society pays every day to build noise walls, that society pays every day for health care for people who suffer from this. that's not what we're talking about. suddenly we are talking about what industry needs to do to adapt, whereas this is about innovation to make it internationally competitive. we're not talking about that anymore. the commission's proposal is apparently over-ambitious. i can rarely catch the commission on that, but in this proposal they would all of a sudden be advocating the end of the car industry? the negotiations on the multiannual budget of the european union, or rather on the distribution of the tax revenues of the european citizen, will continue from tomorrow. you are hereby summoned to the chorus: we need money! but the question for the citizen is: what money? there's no money at all. why else do you think all countries should be cutting so much? the crisis has left us broke! what the european union wants is to take money from the hardworking citizens and from future generations, who are saddled with huge debts. you call that solidarity. i call that immoral. this whole budget of the european union is a monster, the bulk of which is wasted on outdated cohesion policy and terrible agricultural policy. what about the european civil service? there are as many as 5,000 civil servants around who earn more than our dutch prime minister. it is a scandal! no penny to be added, as much as possible to be taken out of the budget. the less money goes to the european union, the better it is for citizens in the member states.
tomorrow is the start of the summit on the european multiannual budget and i call on the heads of state or government to build up confidence. this is the chance to restore the credibility of the european union. a credible european union is a european union that is future-proof. a union that puts innovation and strengthening competitiveness at the forefront. that is the precondition for promoting employment again. i would like to mention two concrete examples of future-proof and future-oriented measures: the european union is investing in a new sea lock at terneuzen. - i'm going to be fine. the european union is going to invest billions in a new graphene development programme. that is the way it should be, that is forward thinking, that is a credible european union with added value. the question is whether all heads of state and government will be so impressed. the european union is a reserve fund for some people, alongside their own national budgets. agricultural and structural funds are often used to supplement their own wishes and that does not help the european union to move forward. that is the way too often budgets are handled. that does not strengthen the confidence of the citizens in europe. the fact that parliament is also considering using a communication budget of €2 million to influence twitter discussions on the european union in a positive way is counterproductive in this respect. criticism of the european union and the european parliament should not be countered by a barrage of positive propaganda, but by learning from and acknowledging and correcting mistakes made. that's building trust. it is necessary!
"blue card" question. what is it? our interpreters always do an excellent job and i think i have heard the interpretation correctly, namely that mrs sousa is advocating that the budget should provide for access to health care for the elderly. i heard that part of your text. europe must not interfere in everything, let us concentrate on the things europe must do. i do not think that it is the task of europe to regulate access to health care for the elderly, however difficult it may be for them, and i readily admit that. but we don't have to do that here.
it has already been said by a number of people that it is not just the size of the budget that is important. i do not think we can have a budget that is in deficit every year forever. we are doing exactly what the member states are not doing, or are at present doing in their own member states at the insistence of the european commission. in addition, a number of points are important that do not cost money. i too believe that a new system of own resources must be introduced. the present system, where we make commitments and then the member states refuse to make the money available to pay those bills, is not acceptable. each year the european court of auditors issues a negative statement on the reliability of the accounts. that's got to change. this can only be achieved if there is greater political responsibility in the member states, and that means that a member state declaration is needed to ensure that the money is better spent.
after all that's been said, i've got four points left. the first point is accountability of financial resources. as mr mulder has said, it is unacceptable, also for the citizens of europe, that we are not able to justify the expenditure of the european union through the national management declarations. the second point is innovation. we believe that innovation should also be given a much greater prominence in the traditional budgets for agriculture and regional expenditure. less transfer costs, more innovation. the third point concerns flexibility. we need to have more flexibility in that multiannual budget so that we can respond to current developments in europe. and the fourth point, after consideration, is a seven-year multiannual financial framework. there's too much uncertainty in those annual budgets. you can't do big projects if you have to do them year after year. and why are we doing all this? to give hope to the youth of europe, to protect the social market economy, to create a sustainable economy which also promotes the values of europe for the next seven years.

the european investment bank has been in existence for over 50 years. during that time, she has provided credit to a multitude of projects. the bank has never been asked to help because of disappointing growth rates in the member states. but in the last few years, it's happened. with the aim of european unification, the bank has been asked to finance many more projects and thus to take greater risks. the capital outstanding is now 395 billion, a sixfold increase in a few years. it's all based on a banking license, so compared to all those investments, there's very little equity. i wonder if the risks involved have been properly weighed. the continuing call for the european union itself to become a shareholder in the bank is the worst of all fears. a political body that hijacks an independent bank to fund its political goals, that can't go well. my group will therefore vote against this report. the european investment bank is not designed to help failing governments out of the fire. if the eurozone is dismantled in a sensible way, the investment in the southern member states, which is now lagging behind, will be restored. the hard decisions must be carried out: a small government, low debt, low taxes. it is also unwise that 10% of the uncovered, almost 400 billion, is invested outside the european union. the contribution to the foreign policy objectives is questionable, not least because the foreign interests of the member states are not identical.

i'm the reporter. - mr president, i would first like to congratulate mrs viviane reding, vice-president of the european commission, not only for her presence but also for the constant attention she pays in her work to the problems of equality between men and women. you will understand, mr president, that i am particularly angry that no member of the council has deigned to come to this debate in this house. you have said very well just now, mr president, that this is a matter of a violation of dignity, a matter of a violation of human rights, a matter of zero tolerance also - you said it - for violence, but also for wages, for the careers of women, for discrimination in employment and all the areas where the crisis has a very negative impact on the situation of women. i hope the council will hear the conclusions of our own-initiative report. i would like to thank mr gustafsson, chairman of the committee on women's rights and gender equality, and all the fictitious rapporteurs for the work they have done. this work goes far beyond the agreed political positions of any particular group. we have been well aware that this problem of equality of citizens is a constitutive value of the european construction. this is a fundamental value of the social europe we are working towards, and therefore no one can turn their back on it. we have thus worked to build a consensus that transcends all political divisions. that is why the debate on 'austerity policy and growth policy' is very much out of date when we are dealing with this subject. we must build together an equal pay system for recruitment and an equal standard of selection. we can see that today there is a real inequality in wages. in equal positions and with equal competences, there are 17% fewer women, but in addition - and you know this very well, madam commissioner - with each new child born, this inequality grows. the last oecd report on the issue emphasized that it was always 12 percent less wages for women, for every child born. a career issue is therefore added to the salary issue. the flat career of women means that, at the end of working life, women earn about 30% less than men. this leads to a real political, economic and social problem in all the member states, since we have a real impoverishment of women. finally, the precarious nature of the contracts on which women are employed, together with the low wages, means that the crisis is now leading to a silent withdrawal of women from the world of work. this is totally unacceptable in the europe we want. this is why the report advocates common criteria for steering, at the level of the european commission and at the level of the member states. we want to see the situation of widening social inequality between men and women stopped today, through corrective policies, and particularly through the european social fund, and to see an end to this increase in the crisis we are experiencing.
- i'm the reporter. - to make the door open immediately, i quote the headlines in the netherlands and other countries: "a repugnant proposal from liotard". why do people get so worked up? because they think i'm trying to ban porn with this proposal. firstly, i can assure you that the report is not intended to impose any obligations or prohibitions, and secondly, it is the result of a process in the committee on gender equality. all my colleagues with a little common sense know all too well that this is not a legislative proposal, and that the purpose of such a report is primarily to draw attention to a problem and to advise and ask the commission to take this into account in future policy. the real issue is that stereotyping still contributes to the fact that: 1) there is a gender pay gap in the same occupation of an average of 16.4% in the eu; 2) that in the netherlands, for example, women earn on average 20% less than men because of the phenomenon that typical women's occupations are paid less than typical men's occupations; 3) that as a result, women are much more likely to fall into poverty later in life. older women are particularly the victims of the wage gap, with disastrous consequences for their pensions. they are more likely to end up in extreme poverty. 4) that it is a fact that one in four women still experiences physical, psychological or sexual violence in her lifetime; 5) that trafficking in women still exists; 6) that in decision-making bodies such as politics, the commission and the ecb, in the management of large and small companies and organisations, the majority is still male. let us not forget that it is not only women who suffer from stereotyping, but also stereotypes based on age, migration status, sexual orientation and disability are still a major problem in the eu. it is also a fact that stereotyping and stereotyping of men and women begins in childhood. the media, but also educational role models, policy makers and parents themselves can help to reduce negative stereotyping, of course with all respect for freedom of expression and media freedom. i therefore urge these people in particular in my report to be aware of the existence of stereotyping in the future and to stop setting and encouraging stereotyping. this can be done, for example, by avoiding the objectification of women, for women are more than that. or by really letting children choose a profession they want to pursue later, without labeling certain professions as either women's or men's. or not always portraying women as taking care of household chores, but also showing that men can do that very well. furthermore, by providing key players and people working in sectors that can influence image formation with tools that help them to cope with the phenomenon of stereotyping, to recognise it and thus to avoid it. these are the points on which the report is based. it is not that i want the report to be adopted because i am the rapporteur. the point is that it is a message that a majority of the committee on gender equality voted for and a message that is important for the future where men and women do not have to be the same but are equal. president. - many women in north africa hoped that the uprisings would mark the beginning of an improvement in their situation. nothing could be further from the truth. during the north african uprising, female protesters have been subjected to sexual harassment on a regular basis. in egypt, this affects as many as 80 percent of all women. article 2 of the new egyptian constitution reads: islam is the state religion, the principles of sharia are the source of legislation. and we know what the sharia law says: gender inequality, violence against women. the sharia even legitimizes the stoning of women. in other words, women in egypt are worse off than before. furthermore, women's participation in politics has not improved at all, according to figures from the united nations. in fact, the tunisian parliament has two fewer women deputies after the elections, and in egypt the percentage of women fell from 12 to 2 percent. the reality, mr president, is that the islamists have come to power. and we all know: in islam, women are inferior to men. in islam, oppression and violence against women are commonplace. where islam is the rule, women are in a bad way. that's the sad truth. thank you, sir.
mr president, the women of north africa took the lead during the arab spring. they were in the forefront, demanding democracy, social justice, freedom and equality. today, women have become the forgotten group of the arab spring. their current struggle is about not losing the rights they had under the dictatorships. ironic and sad, but true. the eu’s contribution is crucial for the setting up of a truly democratic political culture involving gender equality. this requires us to take a coordinated and comprehensive approach. we must make it clear that aid is conditional on progress in democratisation and the improvement of women’s rights. in the meantime we must support the development of civil society. we do not have unlimited time to take action. if the constitutional process is completed without gender equality being effectively safeguarded, the tools we have at our disposal will not help.
vice-president of the commission. − mr president, please allow me to thank the overwhelming majority of this house who have, across party lines, taken the same approach. the fact that women do not have an equal position in the economy, in politics or in society is a great shame, as i think we all agree. at this time, when the crisis and the problems which many families are facing is at the top of the agenda, i would like nevertheless to underline that promoting gender equality is good for growth. all our studies have shown this. a company which promotes equal pay will attract female talent. that is an efficient business model. a company which attracts female talent in top management has better financial results, as has been shown by all the studies. these better financial results then help to create jobs. the proposal before you for ‘women on board’ is also about unlocking talent: the 60% of university graduates who are female and whom we are leaving idle. can we afford that? no, we cannot. that is why i would advise some of the members of this house to read what the commission has put on the table. it is about qualification and talent and the fact that the best candidate should be selected even if she is a woman, which is not happening today. there is a lot of untapped potential in our economy. one thing is very clear, and has been said by many in this house. the crisis is having an impact on both men and women, and is having a greater impact on women than on men. before the crisis women were catching up. the employment rate went up from 55% in 1997 to 62% in 2007, a 7% increase in ten years. the crisis has halted this trend. that is precisely why in the 2012 european semester – and this will be repeated in 2013, but i can only speak to you about what has already happened – nine member states received a country-specific recommendation about affordable care services and about ensuring the fiscal treatment of second earners allows women to continue to work and does not prohibit them from continuing to work. the majority of these recommendations concern the provision of adequate and affordable care services; a second group concerns improving the fiscal treatment of women, in particular, and a third group addresses the question of tackling the gender pay gap. i should like to read you one sentence from the integrated guidelines for economic and employment policies. guideline 7 states: ‘work-life balance policies with the provision of affordable care and innovation in work organisation should be geared to raising employment rates, particularly among youth, older workers and women, in particular to retain highly-skilled women in scientific and technical fields’. as you see, we have included everything that you have called for in our legal basis. the question is how much it is implemented in member states. as you know, we are monitoring the member states’ implementation of this. many specific questions have been raised, and it would take too long to answer all of them in this house. however, there was a very precise question on the recast directive, that is the directive on equality in the labour market and on the pay gap. this directive contains provisions on equal pay as well as provisions on sanctions. member states can provide for sanctions in cases where equal pay rules are infringed. the commission is monitoring the implementation of these provisions and will report on the implementation of the recast directive early this summer. i would like to underline once more some of the figures which mrs morin has put before us, because they are very important. one of them is particularly shocking: women’s average income goes down by 12% with the birth of each child. children should be a source of life and wealth, not a source of poverty. unfortunately, the present situation will result in age-related poverty for women. that will be a tremendous problem for the next generation if we do not do our homework today. when i say ‘we’ i mean the 27 member states. this is something which worries me a great deal. the direct link between bringing up the next generation and suffering poverty in old age is a very shocking development in our society. some members have called for us to promote gender balance in politics. the eu has competence as regards gender equality in employment and in social matters, which is firmly established by article 157 of the treaty. it does not have competence as regards the political and constitutional systems of the member states. however, the european parliament, the elected members and the political parties do, so let us all direct our efforts to where the capacity to change the situation exists. let us ensure that, for the next european elections, these deficits are eliminated. i would also like to reply to one of the points made by reaffirming that human rights are universal and equality is a human right. this issue is high on the agenda of my colleagues mr füle and baroness ashton, who deal respectively with neighbourhood policy and external action, and they always draw the attention of the governments concerned to its importance. finally, i would just like to say a word of appreciation for the rapporteurs – mrs morin-chartier, mrs liotard and mrs costa. their proposals on the labour market, on the economy, on stereotypes and on women in other parts of the world will be the basis of our future work. i would like to thank them very much for the very important work they have brought before us. et vous avez bien mérité des femmes.
i'm the reporter. - mr president, commissioner, i thank you for what you have just said. i would draw five lessons from this afternoon's debate. the first is that the crisis is a great weakening factor for the situation of women today, and i would not like us to imagine that women's rights are constantly advancing. no, history has shown us that there have been periods of sad setbacks and i believe that today we are in a period where we cannot allow this fragility to develop. as for the second lesson, no, the women's workforce is not a supporting workforce. no, women are not an adjustment variable in our society. i therefore thank you, commissioner, for speaking as you did, because we must all work together to ensure that women's right to work is respected, which goes hand in hand with their right to independence. the third lesson is that families and states are investing heavily in girls' education. today, girls are educated as much as boys, if not more. what is scandalous is that the governments do not make efforts to integrate these girls, on an equal footing with boys, into the world of work. the fourth lesson is that all levels must be involved: the public and private sectors, the european level - as we have seen - but also the national levels, which must be stimulated in this area, and finally the local levels, because we know that local implementation must also be encouraged. there are the sayings and there are the facts. we see that, despite all that we say, we have to give a boost to our desire to see these objectives materialize at national level. i'd like to end with my fifth lesson, because it's extremely important. i call on everyone to vote, because what is important is that our vote tomorrow should be a lesson to the council, which is not here today. would not have been possible if not for the efforts of everyone involved. the report i was rapporteur for is very important for the reporting obligations of all member states in the eu on the amount they emit and what that looks like exactly. it is important to ensure that everyone uses the same uniform, transparent rules for the recording of greenhouse gas emissions. what is also important, particularly in the longer term, is that member states should now also start to present the plans for the longer-term transition of their economies to an economy which emits 80 to 95% less greenhouse gases. that transition sounds far away, we're talking about 2050. but the changes will have to happen tomorrow and the day after tomorrow to achieve that in 2050. and these reporting obligations ensure that member states show how they are aligning their measures. another important subject in this dossier was shipping. it was found to be unacceptable for the member states to talk about this at the united kingdom, but it is also germany. the council is quite clear on this point, and even the reporting obligation on: "how much money do we give to climate aid", even that reporting requirement was a big, big problem for member states like the uk and germany. so every time they make a nice display of how green they want the economy to be, but as soon as it becomes concrete and it's about year". and everyone who goes to the international climate talks knows how important this is for developing countries. what is the eu doing to help the climate? every year, it's a big fight. and every year the eu, being the eu, cannot make clear how much money we give. each member state wants to be clear about how much money they give. like the last time in doha: sweden gave a little, denmark, germany, france, the uk. but we couldn't add up, because everyone uses different rules and different calculation methods, and so we couldn't clearly show as the eu how much money we give to developing countries. and that is a problem for international negotiations every year. fortunately, this report will put an end to this, but it took an incredible amount of effort to get germany and the united internationally. this report was all about reporting those steps. if that is difficult, we still have a long way to go, and that is unfortunately the conclusion of this report. we're taking another step forward, we've made some gains again, but we still have a long way to go on the road to that true green economy. the reporting obligations are now in place. now for the real action.

i would also like to say a few words about the income supplements. there's a tendency everywhere to say, "they must be equalised as much as possible throughout europe, because that is a matter of justice". i don't agree with that. it is a compensation to the farmers for the efforts they make, which go beyond what the world market requires. i am not sure that the commissioner has been able to give us a clear answer to this question. it's a payment for services rendered. the price of land in the netherlands is at least eur 50 000 per hectare. a price of land in eastern europe or elsewhere in europe is not nearly as high. so if there's a fee to be paid, i think it should be related to the customer's price. and that automatically means that in certain countries the income supplements will always have to be higher than in countries where agriculture is less intensive. so equalization, if it's fair, is good, but in this case, it doesn't seem to me to be the right key to the distribution.

the european union is not only a political force, but also a political force. they warn of an imminent regional catastrophe. the key words are the long-standing internal islamic dichotomy between sunnis and shiites, and cross-border jihadism. in the meantime, international media are reporting that european countries such as france and britain are training syrian militias on jordanian territory, and at the same time croatian newspapers are reporting that large quantities of weapons have been sent to syrian militias from zagreb airport for months. madam high representative, are these media reports based on fact? and if so, how does this involvement of member states and a future member state relate to european policy towards the hotbed of syria? i do not need to say that i find the humanitarian situation very dramatic. the most shocking thing i've seen recently was a syrian christian woman, i think in her 30s or 40s, who had a cross literally driven into her mouth. the sooner this terrible catastrophe is ended, the better. let us make a real effort to reach a political agreement, but that cannot go hand in hand with military intervention.
mr president, i hope that mrs ashton can answer my question, because what i saw yesterday is that you had an external affairs council meeting and you spoke with russia on a constructive dialogue or engagement. did you also speak on the syrian question and on the delivery of arms and on the un security council? that is more important for me than only having constructive engagement about syria. secondly, you did not answer what we already asked for in parliament in our resolution, which is safe havens for all the people who are now on the move in syria. three million citizens there are no longer living in their own houses. thirdly, the delivery of non-lethal weapons: france, the uk and the united states all promised this, but is there anything already done? those would be my questions to mrs ashton.
mr president, let me start by offering my condolences on the death of ahmad shihadeh, a policy officer at the eu representation in damascus. sadly the days when the deaths of individuals in syria make european news are rare. even as people are murdered by tens of thousands, we are still searching for solutions. while millions are on the run and countless are wounded and missing, what is the value of a human life? commissioner georgieva wrote today that we need a political solution, yet many, including european citizens, are taking up arms and joining extremist networks that take the space that is left by the international community. the people in syria are increasingly crushed between the murderous regime and violent extremists. today, opposition members even took to the streets to demonstrate against al-nusra. it is a huge mistake to pretend that all opposition consists of extremists. we as the european union must get aid to the people and take the initiative for a un mandate enabling direct cross-border operations from turkey, jordan and lebanon. let us get more deeply involved in what happens in the refugee camps, so women do not continue to be abused and extremists cannot infiltrate. we need a strategy for the day after assad – in a country full of weapons, with deep wounds, where there is such a desire for revenge in an ethnically-mixed society. concretely, the eu should also take in more syrian refugees itself. we need to put hezbollah on the eu’s terror list and we must do more to push russia and others blocking a real political solution to finally take a stance and stop the killing of innocent civilians in syria.
- i'm the reporter. - president, commissioner, ladies and gentlemen, the new chinese party leader, xi jinping, says he dreams of a strong china, 'a harmonious, socialist, modern country, on the road to the great rebirth of the chinese nation'. i do not know to what extent the european institutions are part of his dreams, but if that is the case, i would like to make one thing clear first. it concerns recital v of the report. in the drafting of my report, parliament's services have wrongly suggested that the european parliament is embracing beijing's 'one china' policy. this point will be corrected after my report has been accepted. i'll come back to the dreams of xi jinping. as a sober dutchman, i don't have much of a political dream. they have caused too much misery in china and on our continent. but it is true that my report was intended to contribute to the successful partnership between the eu and china. one of the key elements of such a successful partnership is the obligation of mutual responsibility. beijing can expect from us - and i am thinking of the european council - a clear, consistent and unanimous policy. we also call for a responsible policy on the part of china towards its neighbours. neither china nor europe benefits from escalation in the south and east china seas. but this is not the only point i wanted to make in my report. it is my task as rapporteur to give voice to the dreams of chinese citizens. i believe that it is not enough for the eu to focus exclusively on stability and trade. i have to think about the dream of chinese citizens about free speech. in china, whistleblowers are still dealt with extremely harshly. for example, there was a recent tweet that called on local authorities to swim across a heavily polluted river so they could experience firsthand how bad the situation was because of a nearby factory. i'm thinking of the dream of the chinese best-selling author yu hua. he dreams of a china where food inspectors are as strict as film censors and where film censors are as lax as food inspectors. "because", he said, "you have freedom of speech and food security all at once". i also think of a chinese mother's dream of a baby she was expecting. we must not remain silent as the ruthless scourge of the one-child policy destroys the dream of this woman, the child in her womb. i think - to give one last example - of the dream of visitors to the many house churches in china to come together freely and unhindered without interference from the chinese authorities. i therefore urge all the european institutions to remind the chinese authorities, in line with the report we are discussing tonight, that the dream of xi jinping can only be realised if a bridge is built to the dreams, the many dreams of chinese citizens.

member of the commission. − mr president, allow me indeed to offer my congratulations to mr belder on his work, which has led to such an inspiring debate. in closing, i want to reiterate my gratitude to the european parliament for its support for high representative ashton’s work and the focus that members have given to the european union-china strategic dialogue in the report and discussion today. the european union will use the opportunity of engagement with the new chinese leadership this year, not least at the 16th european union-china summit, to map out the further course of the relationship over the coming years. this report gives us the european parliament’s perspective on what is important for it. high representative ashton expects shortly to travel to china for her first encounter with the new chinese leadership. there we will lay the foundation for the next european union-china strategic dialogue, which is due to take place in brussels this year. the european external action service will continue to keep the european parliament – in particular its delegation for relations with the people’s republic of china and its subcommittee on human rights – regularly informed about all developments in european union-china relations.

mr president, we are talking about a specific form of organization: the european mutual society. the commission already proposed a proposal in 1991, which was withdrawn in 2006 because the member states could not agree on it. and that last part is not strange. there is no clear legal definition of a 'mutual society'. there are differences in member states in terms of tradition, history, markets, governance and regulation. there are even countries where intermarriage is not permitted. 'mutual guarantee societies' are not mentioned by name in the treaties, so that article 352 tfeu must ultimately be the legal basis for them. and that means that the member states have a veto. the council will therefore always reject a proposal. so a new bill is a stillborn child. the commission has already made a number of proposals, and i am sure that the commission will be able to accept them. so this is a waste of time. time which we could much better spend on improving our subsidiarity test or on preventing the endless expansion of the powers of the european union. i am thinking in particular of article 114: the commission has already taken a number of measures to combat fraud. that article is being used in an inappropriate way to further restrict the freedom of member states and the european parliament should no longer be involved in that. president. - the next item is the report (a4-0173/97) by mr de gucht, on behalf of the committee on economic and monetary affairs and industrial policy, on the proposal for a council regulation (eec) on the common organization of the market in bananas. the european mutual society can therefore be safely forgotten. (the speaker is prepared to answer a 'blue card' question (rule 149 (8) of the rules of procedure)).
"blue card" is the answer. i think this only confirms my point that member states have great differences in terms of tradition, history, markets, governance and regulation. the 17 million people you are talking about are already covered by a mutual society and they can be very satisfied with that. i agree with the edf spokesman who says that the member states can arrange this properly among themselves. there is no need to extend this structure, which would force member states which have no tradition of doing so to harmonize on the basis of european legislation.
mr president, i am concerned about the integrity of sport, i am concerned about the safety of our citizens and i am concerned about the lack of concrete action, since organised crime is infiltrating sport in europe. the shocking results of the investigation by europol, which were presented recently, have revealed a widespread network of criminal organisations that have been involved in fixing hundreds of football matches across europe. match-fixing is a new form of crime with high revenues and low sentences and detection rates. it is used as a tool by criminal organisations to make and launder money from activities such as drug or human trafficking by gambling on fixed matches. we know about the efforts of the european commission to address match-fixing. however, today, we would like to hear from you, commissioner. how are you planning to address, now, the involvement of criminal gangs in european sports competitions, which is threatening the integrity of sport? you mentioned europol, you mentioned eurojust, but they need tools, they need a definition in criminal law, and they need better european cooperation. some member states are still in denial. those member states are especially vulnerable, since they are not alert and do not perceive match-fixing as a threat. with this attitude, they are creating safe havens for criminals. the commission should take up a stronger coordinating role. we cannot afford to wait to take action until the council of europe is finished with the match-fixing convention. waiting until the end of 2014 is not going to stop criminal organisations. action is needed now. what does the commission intend to do today to protect our citizens and to safeguard the integrity of sports?

in writing. − i voted in favour of parliament’s resolution on the multiannual financial framework (mff) because i reject the european council’s conclusion of 7-8 february 2013 on the mff, as it does not reflect the priorities that are necessary to achieve job creation and sustainable economic development. this notwithstanding, i strongly disagree with the call for the introduction of new genuine own resources and that revenues from a financial transaction tax should be allocated to the eu budget. equally, i do not support the proposal to make full use of the passerelle clause of article 312(2) tfeu, which would transfer power from member state level to eu level. i tabled amendments to delete these parts of the mff resolution, and my vote in favour of the resolution as a whole is not to be taken as support for these parts, but rather seen only from the viewpoint that the european council’s conclusion should be rejected.
in writing. - i am in favour of an agricultural policy which facilitates and stimulates the agricultural sector. in these times of volatile markets, direct income support is still needed. i'll back you up on that. the three greening requirements in the european commission's proposal were a litmus test. it is good that the european parliament has made these three greening requirements more workable. the commission has also proposed that the commission should be given the task of drawing up a report on the implementation of the directive. i support these improvements on the commission proposal. i had expected, however, that the compromise text of the committee on agriculture to make greening more flexible by the option of certification would be adopted in plenary. since that flexibility was not included in the negotiating mandate, i have concluded that i shall adapt my final vote accordingly. although i am in favour of maintaining the agricultural policy, i cannot agree to the negotiating mandate because the greening cannot be implemented flexibly. i therefore have changed my final vote to a negative one.

the commission is also proposing to extend the scope of the directive to include the following areas: i would like to commend our rapporteur, mr othmar karas, for the important work he has done in this parliament and also in the consultations with the council and the commission to reach this agreement, together with the other members. mr president, this agreement contains important priorities for our group. increased capital buffers for banks to help them weather the crisis. extra high buffers for systemic banks, because we want to avoid banks having to be bailed out with taxpayers' money. the commission has also proposed that the european parliament should be consulted on the draft directive on the protection of workers from the risks related to exposure to carcinogens. it is essential that banks can provide loans to smes and this has been rightly taken into account in the capital requirements. this capital requirements legislation is also an important foundation under the new banking union. and with commissioner barnier i would like to say: we've got to get a move on! there is an agreement between the council and parliament to place banking supervision under the european central bank. but how are we to interpret the discussions between the finance ministers in ireland last week? does that mean we are slowing down or are we keeping our ambition? as far as this parliament is concerned, we shall also vote on the agreement quickly and we shall do so. the same applies to the crisis mechanism. i would urge you, commissioner barnier, to come forward with the proposal for a resolution authority for banks before the summer, so that we can complete it before the european elections.
central banks should have been much more vigilant in their control of bank money creation. this is a matter of public interest, because money creation carries with it the danger of inflation. but it is also in the public interest that the best-managed banks survive and that the other banks do not. the new directive mixes monetary policy with economic policy, but monetary policy is and remains the responsibility of the central bank, while economic policy is the responsibility of the bank itself. the new directive will remove the responsibility of banks for their own operations. if a normal company is run poorly, it goes bankrupt or the management is fired, and why should banks be any different? the new directive also does not take account of the differences between member states. it is thus another example of the european union's desire to suppress national legislation and replace it with supranational legislation. mr president, we must reflect on the way the banking sector is run and increasing the banks' buffers is a good idea. a well-run bank would have done that a long time ago. a well-run bank doesn't take irresponsible risks. it is an illusion to think that these european agreements will spare badly run banks from all problems. a more responsible approach to lending could have prevented the current financial crisis, but these new measures restrict free enterprise and have all the hallmarks of mucking up the well after the calf has drowned.

i would like to thank the president-in-office and mr draghi for being here in the plenary of the european parliament. i think the greens also know very well that the ecb has contributed a great deal to the stability we have seen so far, thanks to statements last year that the ecb would always save the euro. that is appreciated, but it is also seen as a danger. you are right in saying that monetary policy will never be able to hold the euro together in the end and that the euro needs more structural support from politics. the ecb cannot continuously, so to speak, intervene in what politics is not doing. one of the major problems could be the independence of the ecb, and that is very important. i would like to speak to you about that, about that independence, because you yourself sometimes put that independence at risk when you make political choices in your statements. let me quote a few. for example, you said in the economic affairs committee of the european parliament last february that austerity is inevitable for countries with high debt. it is strange that you do not mention countries which also have high private debt. you always talk about public debt. why do you not mention countries with high public and private debt, such as the netherlands, which has a combined debt higher than italy? you know that, and yet you never talk about it. is that a political choice you're making on the spot? you also say that the measures must ultimately be to reduce costs, public expenditure, and not to raise taxes. that too is a political choice. why then do you not mention the tax evasion which is taking place in europe, in the eu, on a daily basis? that's not what you're talking about. nor have you mentioned the corporate tax, which has fallen from 35% to 25% on average over the last ten years. these are revenue losses for countries you're not talking about. you only talk about the crisis being solved by cutting spending. that's a political choice you're making. and that threatens the independence of the ecb. but you go further. at the european council last march you even played with figures when you played with mr hollande with labour productivity which included inflation as opposed to wage increases which did not. by playing with numbers like that, france came out very badly and you did that to hollande. that was also a political choice. why are you doing this? the independence of the ecb is very important and you are putting it at risk. why not talk about other factors that drive up the cost? not just labour costs, but also, for example, raw material costs. have you seen the financial times yesterday which showed that the commodity trading houses have increased their profits more than tenfold in ten years? - why? - why? because there is a shortage of raw materials, which means an increasingly high cost for our companies. that cost increase, you're not talking about that. you're just talking about the cost of labor. it's also a political choice. all these choices are dangerous and i ask you if you really want to be a politician, but then you might have to go to another institution. or you will stick very much to your own monetary mandate, but you will not be making choices every time about whether or not to address countries. and if you want to address countries, you could also mention germany's large surplus. for example, you never mention this, although it is also very disruptive for the very monetary union we are talking about. i would like to hear more about that. finally, we attach great importance to democratic control of the ecb as a supervisor, and the european parliament has enforced that. i hope that the ecb will also welcome this very much, that indeed the european parliament exercises a clearer democratic control over the ecb as a supervisor, because this is an additional function. that is no longer just a monetary function, but it is a function that requires additional democratic control. we therefore expect you, mr draghi, to be very clear about the importance of democratic control for this kind of addition to the banking union, which indeed must be completed as soon as possible, but with democratic control.
the ecb's core task is to ensure low inflation. low inflation creates consumer confidence and thus growth and jobs. it's been a tried and tested recipe for decades for countries like germany and the netherlands. this is a fundamentally different approach from that used in the southern countries, where high inflation was used to evaporate government debt. the ecb is currently ignoring this core task. it may be legal, but it was never intended to buy up government debt. the balance of payments - target ii - was never intended to run so high, and monetary policy was never intended to combat the imbalances of the euro zone. the european council in madrid in december last year stressed the need to strengthen the european monetary system and to strengthen the european monetary system. that means, mr president, that the member states must be brave and put forward a plan to dissolve the euro zone. that, mr president, is the only solution.
rapporteur. − madam president, i would like to start by thanking the european court of auditors, the agencies, my colleagues, shadow rapporteurs and the secretariat of the committee on budgetary control, including stagiaires, who worked very hard on all the reports on agencies and joint undertakings. we all know that this debate does not always attract the most attention in the house, but we have to be very clear that it is a very important political debate. it is the only time of the year when agencies and joint undertakings undergo public scrutiny of their activities. we should not underestimate the political power that we have as a parliament in this task. i noticed the political power that we exercise when i tried to persuade agencies to improve a lot of things that they are working on – their management, procedures, internal rules, personnel policy, etc. the fact that we are able to refuse their discharge is a huge power. when i started on my reports, i decided to focus on three elements in particular. first of all, the effectiveness of agencies – whether their overheads are not too high, whether the administrative burden they are undergoing is not too high and whether we do not have too many small agencies that are just not fit for their task simply because they are too small. secondly, i focused on the independence of agencies. there is a reason why we have agencies and do not just let the commission take these tasks. they have to be independent: independent from the commission, from the member states, but also from industry. i focused on that as well. thirdly, conflicts of interest. this was one of the main elements we discussed last year, and it will be discussed further in the ayala sender report. let me focus primarily on a few individual agencies and especially on the environment agency. in a few weeks’ time, the current executive director will step down after being in office for ten years. she has an incredible track record. her personal authority might even be greater than that of the agency itself. she has built the environment agency into an example of how to run an agency. year after year she collected positive audits and positive reports from this house, but last year we saw a radical change. we saw a very critical change. in the end we did grant discharge to the agency – with very good reasons – but it was a very difficult procedure. last year’s smoke did lead to an olaf investigation, and now some are saying that, as there is an ongoing olaf investigation, we cannot grant discharge. but is that not going round in circles? first we create smoke, then the smoke leads to an investigation by olaf, then we discover that there is no fire, but still there is an olaf investigation, and now to complete the circle some of us say that we should not grant discharge. i strongly believe that an olaf investigation, in itself, is absolutely no reason for refusing or postponing discharge. it would be absolutely disproportionate if we decided tomorrow during the vote to refuse discharge right now and to postpone it until later on. i therefore strongly urge my colleagues to vote in favour of granting discharge to the environment agency tomorrow.
rapporteur for the opinion of the committee on development and cooperation. - the committee on development and cooperation has even greater difficulties than the other committees in monitoring expenditure. this policy is being carried out far from europe. the quality of the european court of auditors' reporting is beyond doubt and we therefore rely on it. so three points. firstly, the commission will have to do more in the future with indicators that provide measurable results in development policy. secondly, at the same time, the eu member states will have to choose. you cannot cut staff and demand that the eu delegations monitor more closely spending on, for example, budget support to developing countries. the court of auditors rightly notes that more staff is needed, particularly in this area of development policy. finally, it is important to reiterate the court's conclusion that there are fewer errors in development policy, and considerably fewer, than in the spending of some other european union policies.
i would also like to welcome the council here and say that i was impressed first of all by their enthusiastic report. the best thing would be - the proof of the pudding is in the eating - for the council to put its act behind its words and simply answer the questions put to it by the discharge authority, which is parliament. the commission's discharge is a matter for the council. for the umpteenth time, we are having more or less the same debate. the court of auditors has not given a positive statement of assurance and in all likelihood this parliament - and the liberal group will do the same - will give a discharge again. the indicators are not always positive, but we are going to discharge again. i am also behind it, as i said, and why am i behind it? because there are small improvements visible. one of the improvements the commissioner mentioned is something that parliament has been calling for since 2003 and that has been mentioned in almost every discharge resolution: a declaration by the member state. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. it is voluntary and the commission will have to make it attractive for member states to do so. the commissioner has already mentioned it. i have been asked to give my cooperation to the working party. i will certainly do so, albeit in the completely independent position of a member of parliament. it is up to the commission to make proposals in this respect, not parliament. but we will hope that the working group will come up with proposals which will make it attractive for member states to make that declaration and thus ultimately achieve the great objective of the court of auditors issuing a positive statement of assurance.
i would like to start by agreeing with the general remarks made by mr staes, including those on the discharge of the european parliament, and i would like to draw attention to one proposal in particular. every month meps receive over €4000 for so-called general expenses, such as office supplies, magazines, books, home office equipment and other items. you might expect these various expenses to be well controlled. that is not the case at the moment. some meps keep a close record of what they use the allowance for. but there are also meps who have only a rough idea of their spending. i myself save about 30 000 euros a year from the office fee. i also put it back into the ep's coffers, but i have no idea how much money my colleagues send back each year. in short, there is no control whatsoever over the way the compensation is spent. my group therefore proposes that a proper accountability system be introduced as soon as possible. it's the least the taxpayer can expect from us. the other proposal i would like to draw attention to is the discharge of the general budget, section 3 on the commission. other speakers have already referred to the proposals of the s&d group and i have a comment on them. in the committee on budgetary control we agreed that the commission should be asked to undertake a study which takes a closer look at the differences between the remuneration of national and european civil servants. today, most national policy officials are directly or indirectly involved in european negotiations and the gap in competence between them and european officials seems to have narrowed. hence, such a study is not a luxury at this time. i am surprised to see a proposal from the s&d group to scrap this call for a study. it is not clear why, because here too it is a matter of transparency. european citizens have a right to know that their tax money is being spent well. i therefore urge my colleagues and the groups to support such a call for a study.
today we have another interesting glimpse into the kitchen of the european parliament as it discharges the 2011 budget. the number of employees increased by 4%. in addition, 563 civil servants earned more than the salary of the dutch prime minister. the court also has concerns about the control of tendering procedures. in particular, exceptional award procedures are carried out by the directorates-general. read it, it doesn't match. the ep travel agency is not audited for its performance, although the pvv tabled a widely accepted amendment to carry out such an audit. apparently, there's no interest in where the citizen's money goes. europarl-tv continues to broadcast eu propaganda against the wishes of this parliament, while only a handful of eu supporters are still watching. the unnecessary lux film prize of almost 600,000 euros is also happily awarded. the pvv concludes, mr president, that the european union does not make us money, but only costs us money, a lot of money. to paraphrase former prime minister thatcher with the utmost respect: the problem with europe is that eventually you don't have any money left.
- i'm the reporter. - i would like to focus in my closing remarks on the discharge of the european environment agency. we must realize that even the postponement of discharge is in fact a kind of political condemnation. it's going to be in the media, and the adage "where there's smoke, there's fire" will be reinforced everywhere. so we have to be very careful with that. that is why i am so surprised that the ppe group wants to postpone the discharge of the european environment agency. because, on what grounds? it is not based on documents from the european court of auditors or the internal audit service, because they have not found any irregularities. if we did, the discharge of the european environment agency would be a piece of cake. no, the ppe group is doing so on the basis of the simple fact that there is an olaf investigation, and we do not know what it is about. in other words, the ppe group wants to condemn the european environment agency, the director, politically on the basis of rumours. that's the bottom line. if i listen carefully to the arguments, the fact that the olaf report is due in a few weeks is decisive. and that's a reason to say tomorrow "no, we don't give a discharge". what if it hadn't been a few weeks, but a few months? was that a reason for the delay? i find it really incomprehensible and irresponsible the behaviour of a large, no, the largest group in this parliament. i find it incomprehensible that rumours, the fact that a report may be published in a few months' time which gives a negative assessment of the european environment agency, should be a reason for condemning a person and a european institution politically. that this is coming from the anti-european angle is fine, but that this is coming from the ppe group is really irresponsible.
panagiota constantinidou is a courageous teenager from cyprus. she once believed in the eu dream, but no longer does. she feels betrayed by brussels. when i read the hohlmeier report on the 2014 budget for the european parliament, i understand that. an increase of 3.6%. i see salary increases and the budget for civil servants goes from 937 to 979 million. the number of civil servants earning above the balkenende standard rises to 858. - what? the cost to visitors and opinion-makers from third countries is more than 30 million and the cost to the megalomaniac house of european history is doubled to 12.7 million. i will conclude with a quote from panagiota: "i'm really thinking about whether i want to have anything to do with brussels. it's inhuman what they're doing to us". panagiota, your voice has been heard and you will be there because you are brave and honest.
experience shows that if you really want to cut back, you often need external pressure. and commissioner rehn has proved in many of our european member states that it is only external pressure that really leads to major cuts. as has been said before, 2014 is a special year for the european parliament, with extra costs. but it's a special year because of the election. let these elections be the external pressure we need to make a little more progress with the cuts than without it. i am concerned, not so much about whether we are able to carry out our work with a new budget. no, i am much more concerned about the support of the european people for this institution, the european parliament. and it will certainly not be enough to make headlines about the european parliament's own budget being increased substantially. i am therefore, as the representative of the most pro-european party in the netherlands, particularly critical of our own budget.
a new crane has recently been installed in brussels. the so-called house of european history is being built in parc léopold at a cost of over 50 million euros. the construction of this new propaganda temple has raised many eyebrows, particularly here in strasbourg. last month the mayor of strasbourg pointed out that the official seat of the european parliament is in his city. if there is a need for a new centre to report on europe, it is here, in his city. so, last march, the mayor, through the regional newspaper dna, announced plans for a brand-new information center here in the city. the first phase of 2 million is something that strasbourg is willing to pay for, but the second, much more expensive phase of 15 million euros, is something that europe has to pay for. we must not give in to the demands of strasbourg and we must also have one seat for parliament in brussels!
