the crisis in cyprus has once again demonstrated the euro's weakness. it's a horror. europe is stumbling from crisis to crisis, from bailout to bailout, and it's getting from bad to worse. cyprus is already the fifth largest bailout in three years! the european union is continuing to sink deeper and deeper into the euro-marsh. but the eu always manages to make things worse than they already are. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the european union's financial interests are not undermined by the financial crisis. the euro was already doomed, but now even the savings of the citizens are no longer safe. savers all over europe are aware that what is happening in cyprus today could happen to them next time. in the meantime, the question is not whether new bailouts are needed, but when. two weeks later, cyprus is already in need of another six billion euros. slovenia seems to be the next candidate for a bailout. there is simply no end to this euro-hell. with the euro, the european union has brought about a total crisis in europe. just yesterday, the imf released its forecast for the world economy: 3.3% growth in the community. that's great news! but not for the eurozone! here, the imf does not predict growth, but economic contraction. president. - the euro is finished. the euro crisis can only be tackled if european leaders recognize that the euro itself is the problem. if a country can't keep its own pants on, it has to leave the eurozone and devalue its own currency. that is the only option for cyprus. the bailouts are wasted money and solve nothing. the netherlands must stop wasting money on this and, mr president, one good word of advice, save europe from the european union.
rapporteur. − mr president, i am just taking a little more time for all the work that we put into this. the next vote might be one of those confusing votes where you could easily vote in the opposite way to the way you would like to. i would like to emphasise that, if you want to grant discharge to the environment agency – and i urge you to do so – then you have to vote against at the next vote. so, once again, to vote in favour of discharge, vote against in the next vote.
in writing. - i cannot be counted on to give discharge to the european commission. the member states must show more responsibility in spending european money. year after year, the european court of auditors finds that a large proportion of the errors could have been detected and corrected in the member states. the fact that this is not the case demonstrates the need for good member state declarations. if a minister declares himself politically responsible for the correctness of the spending of eu money in his member state, the error rate will undoubtedly decrease. the european commission, which has ultimate responsibility for good administration, does not exert sufficient pressure on member states to issue these member state declarations. the member states, with four exceptions, do not give it sufficient priority. and that is when every euro deserves to be put to good use. shouldn't this be obvious to everyone in a time of crisis? i also cannot agree to granting discharge to the european parliament. the appropriations for 2011 were up 5% compared to 2010. i do not think that is a sound financial policy. such a budget increase, particularly at a time when citizens and businesses in the member states are struggling to cut back, raises the question of whether the european parliament is really representing the citizens in this.
on behalf of the verts/ale group. – madam president, when i talk to civil society organisations in croatia, i am in total awe of what has been achieved for women’s rights, for lgbt rights, for minority rights and for democracy. processes that took about 40 years in the netherlands have taken ten years or less in croatia. in particular, the most vulnerable, the most discriminated citizens who need european values and rights the most have squeezed everything they could possibly get out of the accession process. our rights and our values are, i think, the most beautiful things that we have to offer and they have been grasped with both hands. that does not mean, however, that croatia is a total paradise on earth yet. the work must not stop at accession. in its march monitoring report the commission identified a number of issues that still need work and, while we as a parliament may be very positive about progress made, we should not shout ‘hallelujah’ just yet. therefore we have proposed an amendment to paragraph 3. another important issue we want to add is about the economic situation. after accession croatia will fully participate in eu 2020 and be accountable under the economic governance mechanisms, like any other member state. we are worried about the fall of gdp and about the unemployment rate, which it is just under 20%. if we take croatia seriously as a new member state, we need to express these worries, and i hope you will support this. let me conclude by congratulating our 12 new colleagues from croatia. from my experience, i can tell them that they have been chosen for the most wonderful job there is. i wish them the best of luck. on his efforts. because these are steps in the right direction. i also hope that the action plan on human rights will lead to further positive developments. the same applies to the new legislation on the european parliament has also called for the establishment of a european union-wide network of information and communication networks. earlier this week, i spoke to the widow of one of the victims. she rightly expressed the hope that justice would be served against the torturers and murderers of her husband and his two companions. that brings me to the pressing question, commissioner: does the present protracted process really justify this hope? do you have any information? is there a transparent process here, which you say is turkey on its way to a state of law, which also gives support to christians, the minorities? the widow spoke positively. she had high hopes. but what about this trial? do you have any new information on that? i would add a second urgent question on the same procedure. at the end of last week i heard that the lawyer for the relatives of the malatya murders, erdal doğan, had been seriously threatened with death by one of the defendants! according to your information, commissioner, have the turkish authorities also taken effective measures to protect this lawyer? finally, i welcome the fact that the resolution calls on the turkish authorities in paragraph 33 to take strict and effective measures to combat anti-semitic expressions. i would like to add in one breath the correct finger-pointing by the turkish-jewish publisher, publicist and intellectual rifat bali that the european institutions should tell turkey that it cannot become an eu member state unless turkey decides to combat anti-semitism not with political declarations but with deeds, with the involvement of the judiciary.
article 49 of the eu treaty states that any european state which respects the values referred to in article 2 and is committed to promoting them may apply to become a member of the union. apart from the fact that turkey is no longer a european country geographically, it is in direct violation of the values and principles which we have held high in this house. in turkey, the values mentioned in article 2 such as freedom and equality are far from being achieved. turkey is sliding further and further down the ladder. the kemalism of ataturk, which was to make turkey a modern and secular state, has been destroyed. the day before yesterday, the turkish pianist fazil say was sentenced by the court to 10 months' imprisonment on probation. why would you do that? because last april, he tweeted a verse by the 11th-century persian poet omar khayyam. he would have insulted islam. in reply to my written question, the commission states that it is following the say case with concern. i call on the commission to put its word to its deed and to take strict action against the conviction of the pianist say, another violation of freedom of expression by turkey. how much more must the situation in turkey deteriorate before the commission finally realises that turkey is not european, does not want to be european and will never be european? europe and turkey can work together and trade with each other, but turkey does not belong in the european union. will the commission continue down the hopeless road or will it put words to action and finally stop the accession negotiations with turkey?
last month, turkish foreign minister ömer çelik called on christians who had left turkey to return. that sounds encouraging, but what about the practice for turkish christians? i am thinking of the legal battle that the erdoğan government is waging against the mor gabriel monastery over land holdings. and not just against that monastery, but against several monasteries. many christians are fed up with the growing uncertainty and have already left. the result: churches that are being demolished or converted into mosques, such as the recent aya sophia in izmir. the call to convert even the aya sophia in istanbul into a mosque is also growing. the fear is that if there is a ceasefire between erdoğan and the pkk, christians in eastern turkey will lose their already limited position altogether. they are being squeezed between erdoğan and öcalan. in short, the turkish government is not promoting the position of christians, it is applying the tactics of the verelendung. she's got a lot of time on her hands. the council and the commission should raise this issue in their discussions with turkey.
mr president, first of all i would like to congratulate the rapporteur, ms oomen-ruijten, and thank our group’s shadow rapporteur, mr obiols, for his good work on this. the report supports the opening of five new negotiation chapters with turkey, and this in itself is a sign that we in parliament truly wish to see progress in relations. the report also clearly identifies the areas where a lot still needs to be done. the urgent need for hate-crime legislation and the situation of the freedom of speech are examples in these areas. the strength of this year’s report is that it not only criticises turkey but also recognises that if we really want improvements we should engage in actual negotiations. if we are serious about supporting the reunification process on the island of cyprus, we should be instrumental in having the turkish cypriots participating in the democratic process. therefore, i think it is a very good idea to support the amendment which asked the president of parliament to look into the possibility of having turkish-cypriot observers here in parliament.
on behalf of the verts/ale group. – mr president, my thoughts have been straying a lot to brussels today. on the one hand i do not envy the men from serbia and kosovo trying to come to an agreement that both can explain to their party members and to their media. it must be excruciatingly hard. on the other hand i think they may be proud to be sitting there – to have a chance to make history and do the best thing possible for their people. i am keeping my fingers crossed. it feels a bit weird to debate without knowing the outcome yet – and i heard that the talks will be ongoing this week – but there are of course a number of issues, separate from kosovo, that are relevant to serbia’s accession process. one of the things i worry about is the continued presence of violent hooligan groups in serbian society. when it cancelled the gay pride event last october because of threats from these groups, the government was in fact saying that it does not have a monopoly on violence in serbia, that these groups are too strong and too violent for the government to control and that they pose a threat to the rule of law and to public security. i would like to see concerted action by all relevant authorities there to combat these groups, and i would like to hear from the commission whether this is happening or not. of course the best proof will be a successful and safe gay pride this coming october and a successful and safe football competition this year. on these and other issues serbia has its work cut out for it in the coming year, and today is the start of that coming year. i will keep my fingers crossed.
mr president, today our ears were directed at the talks which are taking place between serbia and kosovo. we hope that an agreement will be reached as soon as possible. in the meantime, important reforms are taking place in serbia. the legislative and judicial systems are changing massively and very fast. there is concern that the judicial reform process has its flaws. this is one of the reasons why the eu needs to remain engaged with serbia and support its reforms. the report also calls for initial and continuing professional training for judges in connection with this massive change in legislation. we also call for these changes in legislation to include alignment to the anti-discrimination acquis. we are especially concerned about the rights of the lgbt, roma and persons with disabilities. to secure their rights, legislative changes are crucial but are in themselves not enough. serbia should also engage in awareness-raising campaigns on these issues in order to change ways of thinking. we shall be following closely and giving our support for as long as serbia continues to make progress towards eu membership.
we are discussing the commission's strategy for the arctic. i must say that i am very disappointed when we talk about knowledge, responsibility, dialogue. in particular, responsibility is referred to as 'low-risk drilling' in the arctic. what exactly does the commission mean by that? i think it completely misses the debate that is going on in the united states right now and the fact that, for example, conocophilips has decided to cancel its 2014 drilling plans. that there are institutions that say: we are not going to drill in that area, in an area that is twice as affected by climate change! where's the idealism? where is the commission's foresight to say: we do not want drilling in the arctic, we want to protect that area and that is becoming an important eu commitment for once, instead of the vague talk about low-risk drilling, which is empty talk. it's not gonna help us.
parliament is angry at the procedure followed in the adjustment of the scoreboard, part of the so-called six-pack, a scoreboard used by the european commission to assess the economic performance of member states among themselves and to punish them if necessary. when the six-pack was adopted, my party was against the curtailment of national sovereignty, because national sovereignty is something to be proud of and not a historical mistake. the scoreboard is also proving to be useless in practice. everyone can see in his own country that unemployment is too high, or that public deficits are too high, or that regulations are too heavy. the commission is not needed for that. but the scoreboard does show that the eurozone is not stable, because germany has a dominant position. it is not germany's fault that the other countries are not competitive. member states which are unable to make their economies competitive in this respect will not be helped with a scoreboard. these countries are not able to compete in the euro zone and should therefore leave the monetary union for the good of their countries and for the good of their citizens. the fact that we are now talking about the procedure of the scoreboard and not about the implications of the transfer of power to brussels is a good indication that in this parliament too it is not about prosperity but about power, and unfortunately the european commission is no different. whether the correct procedure was followed here is not really important. member states must come to realise that they are responsible for the well-being of their citizens and their place in the world.

author. − madam president, the struggle for human rights has moved online. the availability of the internet and new technologies has helped people to access information, to freely express themselves, to assemble and to document human rights abuses, which can then be shared with the rest of the world. governments that seek to retain control are afraid of empowered populations highlighting corruption, injustice and speaking out for justice, freedom and opportunities. the vietnamese government is no exception. it has imprisoned bloggers and democracy activists, and it has given them outrageously long prison sentences. on top of the consequences for individual dissidents and human rights defenders, a new decree on internet management would legalise content filtering, censorship and sanctions by the government against vaguely described ‘prohibited acts’. this decree would oblige internet companies and providers, including foreign ones, to cooperate with the government in the surveillance and tracking of cyber-dissidents. as the eu is also in the process of working on a free trade agreement with vietnam, it is essential that european companies seeking to do business and to create jobs in vietnam will not be met with surveillance and censorship, and are not forced to perform these acts either. these practices do not only have an impact on human rights, but also on opportunities to do business. they must end, and the eu strongly conveys this message today to the vietnamese authorities.
author. − mr president, like the united states, europe has seen its share of terrorism, and we continue to fight it to this day. however, in the same way that we seek to defend our open societies against attacks from the outside, we must defend them against erosion from within. security and freedom are not a zero-sum game. the america we value as a partner – as an ally – betrays its constitution by compromising basic principles, such as the presumption of innocence, and human rights in general. human rights also apply to suspects or to those convicted of the worst crimes. it is those values that distinguish open societies from closed ones. the national defence authorization act marks a low point in american history. if indefinite detention without trial were to happen in, say, egypt, the us would – rightly – be the first to criticise. i am also very alarmed by the fact that the chief defense counsel advised lawyers not to use computers for confidential contact with their clients. surveillance and disappearing files, as well as eavesdropping, violate fundamental rights. we call on the us congress to help president obama to close guantánamo bay prison. democracies and democrats must hold themselves, and each other, to the highest standards. in the words of norwegian prime minister jens stoltenberg, who spoke after the utøya attacks: ‘violence must be met with more democracy’.
president. - i too find it embarrassing to speak today about terror suspects in guantanamo bay, when today is the day on which the victims of the boston bombing are being buried. nevertheless, it is an important and topical subject which is on the agenda today in the context of human rights. mr president, it is clear that the prison at guantanamo bay seriously infringes many of the fundamental principles of international law, and sometimes even flagrantly violates them. i would like to give you an example of the violation of the right to habeas corpus. the americans have gotten themselves into an internal legal wasp's nest where there are really only losers. i'm not so worried about prisoners on hunger strike. it's happening all over the world, and it's a tried and tested way to get attention. that's why we're standing here today. the prisoners have succeeded. i would like to draw attention here to the fact that this is an internal american problem. it is a problem which we must continue to call for, even if we do not have to put our hand in that wasp's nest. it is clear to me that the eu should not function as a haven for ex-prisoners. we have enough problems at the moment with young europeans going to syria to fight. many young muslims will come to regard these ex-prisoners as martyrs and so they may further radicalize young people in europe. the us must try the prisoners and if they do not, they should be released and should be taken in by the countries of organizations such as the arab league, the african union and the oic. these organisations should take their responsibilities, not the eu.
i would like to point out to the colleagues who voted against backloading emissions trading last month a number of consistency problems. one of the reasons why it was said that 'we cannot intervene in the emissions trading system now' is because there is such a problem with emissions trading. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the emission trading scheme is not used to promote the production of electricity. and it was mainly the energy-intensive industries that were opposed to emissions trading. the energy producers are less affected. that proves that we need an additional energy policy. the additional energy policy means: setting a binding target for renewable energy. those who see problems with emissions trading on the one hand, but do not want to set an additional target for renewable energy on the other, have a consistency problem. so if you have a problem with emissions trading - and that was on this side of the house last month - vote now for real energy policy, that is, with a binding target for renewable energy. i am grateful to you, mrs weber, rapporteur of the report we have been waiting for. let us remember that this report is here after long debates on the situation of the media in italy and hungary. we are not limiting ourselves to those two countries, however, because we realise that there are many more european countries where there is something to be gained from media freedom, and we also know that good, honest democracy cannot exist without a free press. we in the european parliament are not in favour of telling journalists what is and is not allowed, of dictating ethics, we should not do that. what we do want is to ensure that they have the freedom to write what they want to write and that there is also a choice for citizens to choose one or the other press, i.e. that there is media pluralism. my question to the european commission is therefore: i am not saying that the commission should not be involved in this, but i would like to say that the commission is not involved in this.
- i'm the reporter. - let me start by saying something else. i have just heard in the dutch cabin mrs ada dingemans, one of our excellent interpreters, who is sadly leaving us, speak in her characteristic voice. i want that to be recorded in the minutes. we're going to miss her terribly. i'm glad i get to hear her again tonight. i would like to thank my colleagues from the committee on social affairs, but especially mr thomas mann, the econ rapporteur, and all my colleagues for the very pleasant and very fruitful cooperation. we all want a secure, sustainable and adequate pension. that is the guarantee and that is what we want for everyone. but pensions remain and are a responsibility of the member states. that does not mean that we as a european parliament have no opinion. pensions are already part of the consultation and designation process within the stability programmes and also the national reform programmes. what is important to me and to my colleagues is certainly the social component. how are we going to ensure that in each member state the elderly have and maintain a decent standard of living? we must ensure that, including in europe. we have an inverted population pyramid right now: i would like to thank the rapporteur for her report, which is a very good report, and i would like to thank the rapporteur for the work she has done. we age. at present, one third of the european population is over 55 years old. we have a great many different systems of old age provision in europe. we have the first pillar's containment systems. they are the perfect expression of solidarity between generations and will continue to be the most important provision in the future. but a population that is becoming increasingly wealthy puts pressure, especially on the younger generation, and also puts pressure on solidarity. the financing of ever-increasing expenditure from the current budget does not only affect the stability pact. in countries where the financing deficit or the public debt is too high, the first priority is always to cut pensions and social benefits. that must be prevented. we must not only work longer - that was the argument - but we must also work with more people, especially those who are now forced to take to the streets. so, ensuring that older people can continue to work, focusing more efforts on flexible career and pension schemes and improving and adapting the conditions for continuing to work. but the first pillar also applies: reserving, reserving and saving. there are member states which have a second pillar, and these are usually pension institutions for which employers and employees have saved together. but there are also pressures, pressures from the financial crisis and the poor performance of the bond and stock markets and of course the rising life expectancy is also a problem there. the european commission now wants to improve the sustainability of the capital invested in pension funds. i think that's the wrong answer. - why? i would like to start by saying, dear ria, that you have done your very best to improve the commission's disastrous pension proposals. you are very clear, particularly with regard to the second pillar of company pensions. you say that the commission must respect the systems of the member states. but why not also that clarity in the first pillar, the aow in the netherlands? we must reject interference there in the strongest possible terms. proposals to link the first pillar to years of work, to raise the minimum wage, to prevent early retirement are not the eu's concern, and that is independent of the fact that i think they are bad proposals. a first pillar pension should guarantee a decent standard of living, especially in times of crisis. however, the report states that a second and third pillar are often necessary for an adequate pension. the eu's interference is not in line with article 114 of the treaty, which states that the eu must refrain from tax provisions and the rights and interests of workers. i am very sorry that you did not say clearly in the first pillar that the eu should not interfere in this.

the european parliament has done a good job. there's a report full of recommendations. i do miss the principle that you pay tax where you have the activities that have led to profit, but the report is full of concrete proposals. the commissioner is also prepared to act. the european council, i have much less confidence in it so far, because i find it vague. information exchange is fine, but that's not going to get us that trillion. i therefore call on the heads of government to make the following points concrete in their action list: 1. scrutiny of all tax treaties, both between member states and with third countries. i would like to ask the commissioner whether he is aware of the fact that the commission has not yet taken a decision on the matter. the second. if money is funneled to third countries, member states must levy a levy so that the overall tax burden is at least 25%. the third. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the tax base is not voluntary, but harmonised so that all member states have the same base and can decide on a minimum rate of at least 25%. 4. other reporting by country of companies on turnover, profits and deferred tax. even the tax inspectors are in revolt at this moment. they've organized a week of action in june. all over europe you see the population mobilizing. the european council must act now or lose its credibility.
finally, the council will also be discussing the revenue side of the euro crisis tomorrow. we have been talking for years about public spending and that something must be done about it to solve the crisis. while we're doing that, the government's revenue is going down. for example, the average corporate tax rate in the eu has fallen to an average of 23%. in 2000, it was well over 30%. that is less revenue which then has to be corrected on the expenditure side. as long as the council thinks it can solve the euro crisis by spending alone, it is choosing a non-solidarity and unfair approach. because the ones who can evade taxes are the rich, the rich who can pay the kpmg to be able to pick all the routes. these are the companies that are now only paying 0.05% of the price, apple for example, as we read this morning in the financial times. that is the problem and the council must now really start talking about lifting the veto in tax matters, otherwise we cannot solve the euro crisis.
we are seeing cuts all over europe at the moment. it is the word of the day and the european union itself is not exempt from it. it is therefore unacceptable that tax fraud can still be carried out on a large scale in europe. what is worse is that some countries facilitate this by still maintaining banking secrecy. that is one of the first things that should be addressed. we must have uniform rules on this throughout the european union, with information being exchanged. the proposals contained in the reports on which we are about to vote can generally be supported. they are encouraging progress. i think that special attention should be paid to vat fraud. it is an important source of income for the member states themselves, but it is also an important source of income for the european union. the system will have to be reformed to ensure that it is transparent and applied fairly in all countries.
mr president, alde, like the other groups, fully supports the fight against tax fraud and tax evasion. however, policies must be adopted in a transparent and democratic way, and the urgency of the matter does not really justify bringing in policies by the back door. there should be no policy laundering. a lot has been said about the foreign account tax compliance act (fatca), and everybody says we welcome fatca. but fatca is a us law that is applied directly on eu territory. i ask this house: if it were not a us law but a chinese or a russian law or a law of any other country, would we also welcome the fact that it is being applied here? the eu needs to have the courage to make its own policies and set its own standards. we have been unable to do so for many years, and we should not need the americans to adopt our laws. finally, i say to the european commission that i asked for access to the fatca documents a year ago and have still not had a reply. all i get from the european commission are delays and an offer to discuss the matter over coffee. i do not want to have a chat over coffee with the commissioner, not to discuss access to documents at any rate. i want a reply. if i do not get a reply i will be obliged to go to court yet again.
the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the community's financial interests are not undermined. when will we have a genuine single energy market in europe, with a genuine level playing field, as we want in the other sectors? that means: the commission has not yet adopted a proposal for a directive on the protection of workers from the risks related to exposure to asbestos. at present, the energy market is far too much nationally controlled, too diffuse and too little transparent. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council and parliament on the subject. and, more importantly, what is the council going to do about it? because if there is one area of competence that the eu should have, it is energy. i think there is too much emphasis on capacity building, which i think is very 20th century. more flexibility, that's an old reflex. and i would like to know what you mean by a particular sentence where you say that measures to combat .... to say that this step, a european supervisor, is incredibly important. it is an incredibly important step that we are now finally working together to have one supervisor for the banks in europe. so important, in fact, that democratic control cannot be underestimated. i have the feeling that this is being treated very lightly, particularly by the ecb. yes, you have to separate the monetary task of the ecb from the supervisory task. but democratic control of this supervisory task is crucial and i am very pleased that in the negotiations my colleague, mr giegold, has also enforced a number of very important aspects for the parliaments, such as the right of inquiry for the european parliament and for the national parliaments that they can also call the supervisor to account. but there are still good agreements to be made with the ecb and in those negotiations i really wonder whether the ecb really thinks that their tasks, those supervisory tasks, should be subject to democratic control. i also have a question for the irish presidency, because they have just said that they had the idea that a number of countries outside the eurozone would already be joining that monitoring mechanism. i would like to hear from the irish presidency which countries are already considering this, because that is not very clear to us yet. the important point is that the banking union is not finished. a bank supervisor may see problems, but it has not broken the deadly embrace between governments and banks. we need to move towards a common resolution fund, but also towards a european deposit guarantee scheme. mr schoible said in the financial times that we should not be too optimistic. we must be careful that this is not used as an excuse to avoid taking the steps which are absolutely necessary in the completion of the banking union.
the european parliament has voted in favour of the commission's proposal to extend the scope of the directive to include the use of the term 'common carrier' to cover all transport services. the present proposal is therefore contrary to article 282 of the treaty, because it affects the independence of the european central bank. parliament is now aggravating this situation by saying that the ecb is accountable to this european parliament. this is yet another example of the violation of the treaty, and thus of the rights of citizens and of the member states, under the guise of the crisis. so this proposal must be dropped, because how can we vote on a proposal which is contrary to the treaty? nor does anyone believe that centralising incompetent national supervisors will improve quality. this is only a preliminary step towards recapitalising the banks through the esm. this proposal is just another ransom note to save the euro.
i would like to thank our rapporteurs, marianne thyssen and sven giegold, and also commissioner barnier for the important work they have done to bring this agreement about. an agreement on ecb banking supervision is an important step towards the banking union. the european central bank will be the new direct supervisor of the major banks in the euro area from 2014. this should help to restore confidence in our banks. restoring the free movement of capital. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the community's financial system is in a position to meet the needs of the world economy. that's crucial to economic recovery. it is therefore necessary to clarify this as soon as possible. the banking union is in the pipeline, but the building is not yet finished. last night we voted in the econ committee by a large majority for a clear approach to crisis management: the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the european union's financial system is not undermined by the failure of the european central bank to provide the necessary guarantees for the protection of depositors under 100 000 euros, and it must be clear throughout europe that investors and ultimately depositors over 100 000 euros are the ones who have to pay when a bank is in trouble, not the taxpayer. we want a credible and a robust banking union. too often, member states are on the brakes. they want european supervision and european crisis management in words, but in deeds they are calling for flexibility and more powers for national supervisors. we risk eroding the banking union. there is still uncertainty about whether our banks in europe are still healthy and which banks are weak. the president of the eurogroup, jeroen dijsselbloem, recently spoke in this house about a study on the quality of bank balance sheets, the portfolios that banks have in terms of loans and government bonds. this examination should take place prior to the start of the ecb. but when will that investigation take place? who will be running the tests and who will decide whether to recapitalise? is that the ecb, the national supervisors, the eurogroup?
today we are talking about a banking union and european financial supervision in the wake of the debt and banking crisis. but unfortunately it will not be limited to banking supervision, given the building blocks for a genuine monetary and economic union proposed by president-in-office of the council, mr van rompuy. last week french president hollande spoke of a future european economic government. there's a disturbing kernel of truth in that. i understand that money is needed to stabilize banks. but is not the emergency fund the appropriate instrument? the commission's proposal is to be adopted by the council at the next meeting of the european council in brussels. this undermines the budgetary rights of national parliaments. the banking union is not the solution to the lack of competitiveness within the european union. i would like to hear from the council whether the use of the exchange rate mechanism is not a better guarantee of a lasting solution. it is to be feared that we are again on a dead end road, another step towards an economic government that europe can well afford to miss.
the current crisis is partly caused by the banks and there are certainly questions about national supervision of the banks. take my country: the netherlands. several large system banks were nationalised. has the monitoring been sufficient? that is the question. another large bank, dsb, quickly went under. with effective supervision, it would probably not have been so, and certainly not so extensive. it is therefore reasonable that the ecb should become the new supervisor and that this supervisory task should require democratic control. this is in contrast to the other task of the ecb, monetary policy. it is crucial that the ecb clearly separates these two tasks. at the same time, the democratic legitimacy of european banking supervision is also its weakness. how can we prevent certain banks from being subject to less or different supervision, especially when they have enough political friends in the various european institutions? who can guarantee that european banking supervision will function better than the present national systems? that is the dilemma, and that is why i support this proposal with a grinding of my teeth. with this tightened ecb supervision, the european banking union is over for me. a european resolution mechanism for the resolution of failed banks and a european deposit guarantee scheme are undesirable, both in substance and in political terms. because if we start wiping out all the debts in one big pile again, then even more people will be voting against when it comes to europe.
i would like to thank the commissioner for his report, which is a very good report, and i would like to thank the rapporteur for his report, which is a very good report. we can all make that list. but we must not only measure the state of the union by the economic crisis and the situation on the markets in europe, but also by the fact that a lot of the debates in europe on ethics, on the rights of citizens, on the accused and on refugees are silent in the council. this parliament has been preparing for years for what is coming soon, namely votes on the common asylum and migration policy. but the council is fighting each other and is incapable of solidarity among member states. the stockholm programme was a precursor to the lisbon treaty. in future we must not set up such large programmes, but we must realise that we must make well-balanced legislation, with a view to fundamental rights and not just criminal law. an intergovernmental system as one big holistic programme, developed piece by piece, is now becoming obsolete. we cannot go any further on this road as long as there is disagreement in the council. i would like to mention as a most striking example the principle of mutual trust in the field of judicial cooperation in criminal matters. the european arrest warrant, which leads to the transfer of citizens from one country to another without proper legal assistance, is typical. we cannot continue to cooperate in the field of justice if we do so in a way that actually reflects mutual distrust. if the council has an answer to what the future should be in terms of this mutual distrust, i would like to hear it.
i ask what is really good about the stockholm programme. the programme opposes the border controls that member states want to be able to carry out to deter criminals. the programme accepts the impossibility of defending oneself against a european arrest warrant and that has already led to years of unjust detention of many citizens in foreign cells. the programme aims at european criminal law, which undermines the sovereignty of member states. and the common asylum policy has not led to a reduction or a dispersal of immigrants, but only to a concentration of them in a few member states. in all this, this programme is not serving the public, on the contrary. the central question must be whether the people are actually served by the stockholm programme and my answer is no. the stockholm programme is a failure. it must be terminated and no new programme must be created.
we had a debate earlier this evening on the stockholm programme, on cooperation in europe in the field of freedom, security and justice. we also discussed how the public perceives europe. we must also discuss this here. how is it possible that the european commission, in cooperation with the shadow rapporteur of the ppe group, keeps saying that secrecy is paramount and that we want to make bits and pieces of it public? the basic principle of democracy should be that everything is open, unless it is absolutely necessary for reasons of public health or social unrest. and that's not the case here. the commission's principle is always 'keep it secret, keep it secret, keep it secret'. and i have to admit that our rapporteur is right, and that leads to mistrust. the european citizens' distrust of the work of the european commission, and also of this parliament and the council, is already quite high. that would not be necessary if we simply show what we do and want to make our documents public. there are a lot of member states that have very good practices in this area. i call denmark, i call my own country holland. the european commission would do well to follow that example. the european parliament has been pulling a dead horse for some years now, as we call it in the netherlands, and that is a pity. it shows that you don't really trust us, or our citizens.
mr president, i would like to congratulate ms sommer, who just did the most perfect impersonation of sir humphrey from the ‘yes, prime minister’ series that i have ever seen in my life. i cannot believe that i have heard someone say in this hemicycle: ‘well, we should not give too much information to the citizens because they would only get confused’. i mean, come on, that is something from a farce on television. it cannot be real, not in this house, because the eu, ms sommer – who has disappeared – is no longer the old project of diplomats where the culture of this question of secrecy prevails. it is rapidly evolving into a mature political union of citizens, and the citizens, ms sommer and dear commissioner, are clamouring for transparency, because transparency is essential for accountability, which in turn is indispensable for a mature democracy. we talk a lot, the commission talks a lot, the council talks a lot – i can see that the commissioner is fascinated (not) – the commission talks a lot about getting the trust – mr president, maybe you could point out to the commissioner that we are having a debate here. we are actually addressing the commission here... (the chair interrupts the speaker in order to gain the attention of the commissioner)...if you care to hear. we talk – the commission talks – a lot about winning the trust of the citizens, yet there is no culture of transparency in the commission. i can testify to that, because i actually took the commission to court in a case where they refused access to documents. i took the council to court and i am very ready to take the commission to court again, and i am pretty sure that this will happen. if i do not do so, dear commissioner, the citizens will do it themselves. have you ever heard of wikileaks? have you ever heard of acta? if you want a demonstration of how secrecy will actually sink your own plans, acta is perfect. i think it is regrettable that the commission and the council take a minimalist approach to transparency and disclose as little as they can possibly get away with. so, commissioner, do everything in your power to unblock this dossier.
blue-card question. – first of all, to eliminate one misunderstanding: i did not say that wikileaks should stand in place of the transparency regulation; i just said that citizens take it into their own hands. i very much subscribe to your statement just now, and i think other colleagues do too, but it rather contradicts the statement that was made by ms sommer just before. i think it would be very useful if we knew what the position of the epp group will be in the end. i hope it will be yours.
"blue card" question. what is it? is it not a great shame that mr eppink compares commissioner rehn with brezhnev? because isn't it colleague eppink who has also actively supported the six-pack? what about the independent role that commissioner rehn now has? and is it not important, now, two weeks before the country-specific recommendations come out, to give full support to commissioner rehn, to come out with very firmly defined recommendations not only for small countries but also for large countries like france? isn't that more important right now? i'm curious to see your reaction.
the plans for the enlargement and deepening of economic and monetary union give the european commission even more power. the plans for national economic reforms must now be approved by the european commission before they can be sent to their own national parliament, and this is another attack on national sovereignty. since the european commission has the right to reject any legislative proposal, this prior approval deprives national parliaments of the right to debate and vote on any legislative proposal sent to them by their own government. no one should be happy about this proposal to strip national parliaments of their powers. we must make ourselves stronger for national representations. how can we accept the disappearance of democracy, which has been fought for for so long in so many countries? the call for greater democracy is heard regularly in this parliament and we have the opportunity to do so again. let us strengthen democracy and reject these committee proposals. this total union is not in the interests of the citizen. the eurozone is the only one in the world that is unable to climb out of the economic valley, because monetary union in its present form is not working. the eurozone must therefore be dismantled and national sovereignty strengthened, so that the member states are again responsible for ensuring prosperity for their own citizens. we do not need more, but less central power. if we do not reject these proposals, the european parliament will show that we are not seeking prosperity but only the centralization of power.
on 7 may, commissioner andor gave an encouraging speech on what we have to do. we need more solidarity, he said, and he quoted habermas: "you help someone because you have a future together". i agree with him. we have now spent three years on more budgetary discipline and more economic agreements. solidarity has been largely forgotten. i welcome the plans for a fiscal shock absorber, for example in the form of an unemployment fund. but the council should start now, not in the long term. i am glad that the commission is finally recognising that we must not only correct the economic imbalances but also the social imbalances. it is a great pity that the commission has been blind to this when drawing up the six-pack. the scoreboard in the six-pack touches on all sorts of social issues: the commission has already taken a number of measures to improve the situation. but the way member states are now evaluated means that economic reforms are mainly about competition, wages and working conditions. that increases social inequality rather than reducing it. i am afraid that two separate processes are now emerging. one for economic imbalances, with tough legislation and tough sanctions, on which member states will actually base their policies. and then there's one for social imbalances with some nice benchmarks that are butter-smooth, sort of an open method of coordination 2.0 that nobody's going to care about. i would like to hear what the commission's plans are. will the social leg be merely a symbolic exercise or will the commission work with parliament on a new integrated socioeconomic scoreboard which does have an eye to combating inequality, poverty and unemployment?
in writing. − i voted in favour of the gáll-pelcz report on the annual tax report, in particular because of the parts included on fighting tax fraud and tax evasion. however, i strongly oppose new own resources for the european commission, which will only cause this institution to become less controllable. i also strongly oppose the call for a ccctb in the form of a 29th regime, which will not solve the problems we see today with corporations avoiding corporate taxes.
on behalf of the alde group. – mr president, the commissioner mentioned in his introduction that he was here on behalf of the high representative, ms ashton. mr commissioner, could you tell us whether she is now at the friends of syria meeting that is taking place in jordan and, if she is not there, why not, and whether she has attended these meetings before? i agree with colleagues that the eu must be much more involved in finding a solution to the violence and the humanitarian crisis in and around syria, because for over two years we have witnessed how syrians have been murdered in the most brutal way. the conflict is rapidly becoming increasingly radicalised and the syrian people are being crushed; a regional disaster is looming. the corpses of boys and men were revealed by the receding waters of a river, hands tied behind their backs, bullet holes in their skulls. children seek shelter in the arms of their mothers to no avail; there are at least 80 000 people dead, and most of them are civilians. of course people are fleeing this horrible violence, and enough is enough, but beyond the death and destruction a humanitarian crisis of unprecedented magnitude is unfolding. there are now seven million syrians who rely on aid, around four and half million are displaced in the country and the number of refugees is expected to rise to three and a half million in the next month. four and a half thousand cross the border to lebanon every day. these people are mostly women and children. they need basic help: food, water, shelter, medicine. girls and women are especially vulnerable. we receive horrific reports of rape, and also of the selling or marrying-off of girls far too young to marry. these girls and women need specific aid. the eu needs a long-term plan, and we must prepare to free up a lot more in emergency aid. the un will ask for no less than usd three billion to cover the needs only for this year. i believe the external action service needs to develop a roadmap for political governance in the liberated areas that includes the possibility of lifting economic sanctions, and we need to seek a un security council mandate that allows for the delivery of humanitarian aid. the assad government is using access to aid as a weapon against its people, even though that is against international law. let us also pressure russia to be more constructive and stop delivering arms, and let us make sure that countries which promise aid do also transfer the money.
we have now heard from you, commissioner, and from my colleagues, a good description of the situation. we all know how badly it is in jordan, syria and lebanon. i was myself on the jordanian-syrian border two months ago, at a refugee camp. a refugee camp so big it's become the fourth largest city in jordan. i wholeheartedly support the pleas here to continue funding unhcr. i would also like to add that unrwa, which is taking in palestinians who are fleeing for the second or third time, should not be forgotten. but the situation in jordan, lebanon and turkey with refugees on the run does not exempt us from reflecting on what this means for the european union. i was very shocked yesterday when we were negotiating the resolution of this parliament on the situation of refugees that we were not allowed to include texts on our responsibility. there are countries in the european union - and i would like to embrace them - that are now directly inviting refugees from syria. there are also countries in the european union that still lock up syrian refugees for long periods of time and still threaten to deport them. we should name it and be ashamed of it. greece is one of those countries, and it is not enough to say that the country is in crisis. there are signals from unhcr that boats with syrians are being pushed back into turkish waters. in the meantime, the european union has a fund to help them resettle refugees in major crisis operations. but that fund was not even used for the situation in libya! when will we be able to use that fund? is it not time to take responsibility for the syrians who are already in europe (who should not be in detention, who should be helped) and also for the refugees who have no place in lebanon and jordan, for example because they are palestinians or because their political situation is too complicated?
"blue card" is the answer. it is always nice to be able to talk a little further about this very problematic case. the representative of the unhcr in greece, in athens, informed me a few days ago that he was receiving signals from refugees who were unable to reach greece and that syrians seeking asylum in greece were also being put in prison for 12 months in the first instance, precisely because they were seeking asylum. that does not seem to me to be the right course of action at the moment. you might say: the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them.
for the epp group commissioner, before i focus on the plight of the syrian refugees, i would ask you to make an urgent effort to secure the release of four syrian churchmen who have been kidnapped. i'll give you their names in a moment. i was just talking to a syrian acquaintance and friend of mine, and he said that the horrible rumor was going around that one of them had already been crucified by chechen jihadists. i would like to know, commissioner, what your views are on three distressing aspects of the gigantic syrian refugee problem in neighbouring countries: i call them: - the infiltration and influence of the warring parties in the camps (i am thinking of recruitment, but also of extrajudicial executions, in jordan for example); - the vulnerability of adult women, but also of underage girls, to the physical exploitation of their immense suffering (survival sex is even mentioned, a horrible word); - the systematic discrimination - also documented - against syrian refugees of palestinian origin. the commissioner will no doubt be familiar with these three concrete examples of the profound suffering of syrian refugees. but how is it possible that these abuses took place in the camps and are taking place under the watchful eye of the international community? what is the eu's role in this?
first, a technical point: i am not speaking on behalf of the non-attached members, i am one of the non-attached members. there are no winners in a civil war. it is not a national one, and in the case of syria, it is not an international one. it is difficult to say which of the warring parties that will ultimately prevail will prove to be the most reliable. stability in the region, where the jewish state of israel is threatened on all sides by regimes bent on its destruction, is of vital importance to all actors. on the one hand, there are the opposition forces, who claim to be fighting an iranian-backed jihad, and on the other hand, the assad government, which is now being fanatically supported by the hezbollah terrorists. today the german foreign ministry announced that it wants hezbollah on the european union's terror list in the wake of the horrific terrorist attack in burgas, bulgaria. the inclusion on the eu terrorist list comes much too late, but better late than never! how does the european commission view this german proposal? i'd like a response.
the syrian civil war is taking apocalyptic forms. it's not just a battle between assad and the opposition anymore. violence is increasingly perpetrated along sectarian lines, leaving vulnerable groups virtually helpless because they receive no foreign support or financial resources. christians in particular are among that vulnerable group. in fact, you can already see that there is a kind of "axe day" coming, where vulnerable groups are killed and driven out of revenge or frustration. we've also heard about rapes that are taking place. in fact, you're seeing the same thing happening as what's already happened to christians in iraq. that is why i ask mr fühle: 1. what will you do to protect the most vulnerable groups in syria? 2. how will you get all the aid to the victims in areas controlled by assad and the opposition? we must do everything we can to prevent the conflict from degenerating into sectarian slaughter. i'd like to hear your answers.
we are faced with a terrible tragedy in this debate on syria. an international community that is not only hesitant but also internally divided to the bone. a european union which plays no role, partly because it is divided. and the citizen is the victim and is becoming more and more the victim, because summer is coming once again. commissioner fühle says: we'll raise more money. the un is providing more money. but first, a resolution must be passed. political solutions must be found in any case. let us look at what commissioner fühle just said: "we support the lavrov and kerry conference". but has this conference produced any results? the russians just delivered a very sophisticated missile. where are they in this process? assad won't play. the opposition doesn't see it either. and we support that! that's not the solution i'm looking for. let me say a word about the sanctions. the sanctions are due to expire on 1 june. and the british want to supply weapons. how are you gonna put that together? what is the role of the european union? and one last question: if there were weapons to be delivered, who would you do that to? i've already asked you to take an inventory of those in the opposition you'd like to support. we have not received them yet. the european union must try to do something about it, because we can no longer stand by.
madam president, the resolution we will vote on tomorrow will not be the first or the last. since the beginning of the syrian conflict we have had 16 council conclusions, three un security council resolutions, the report of the independent international commission and countless statements by high representative ashton and commissioner georgieva. the syrian conflict created 1.5 million refugees: 350 000 in turkey, 470 000 in each of lebanon and jordan, and 146 000 in iraq. but we are not talking about numbers: we are talking about human beings – human beings caught up in a bloody conflict in their country, who had to leave everything behind in order to seek safety in other countries. they are the families who are being attacked by the locals in turkey following the bomb attacks in reyhanli; they are the women and girls who are victims of sexual violence in refugee camps in jordan. we in europe have been unable to end the syrian conflict, but i am convinced the member states can certainly do a lot more for the refugees. in the end they are not numbers: they are human beings.

picture trickles down and translates to the individual level. i believe we have an opportunity which we must take. but we cannot be naive about the challenges of making it all work in a world that is the potential free trade agreement between the european union and the united states is already being talked about in superlatives, and i also believe that an ambitious agreement can certainly have positive effects in all sorts of areas. i would like to draw particular attention to the opportunities for the maritime sector when the restrictive american jones act is brought to the table. but i also want to make a few comments. european smes are already involved in a wide variety of bilateral trade agreements. the so-called spaghetti bowl effect does not make international trade any clearer. furthermore, an agreement between two players of this size could seriously undermine the meaning of the wto. the eu must continue to commit itself to the completion of doha.
it is very good to have this debate here this morning because there seems to be a kind of tension in this resolution which i think can be removed. the point is that you're providing an opportunity to develop from the bottom up. there's so much quality in the alpine region. i was in singapore recently and the development is going very fast there. i said: "when you look at the world, what do you find most beautiful?" and then they said, the alps! look at austria, look at switzerland. we have a unique quality and we have to deal with it in a unique way, so we must focus policy on it and build on it. however, with the apparent contradiction in article 5, it seems as if the european commission is being asked to prepare such a strategy, even though it is said that this should be limited to a certain area of that convention. that's not the case. that is the challenge facing the commission: i think that the commission should be able to take a step back and to put forward a proposal for a directive on the protection of workers from the risks related to exposure to asbestos. that is why it is better to take out that article, which has been repeatedly mentioned this morning, and ask the european commission to submit a proposal to that effect, so that we can solve the problems. we must not therefore adopt existing instruments as a dogma, but rather take as a starting point the problem we wish to solve. so it may be wise to remove article 5 and thus remove this apparent contradiction. finally, it is good to tackle this policy together now. we have had good experience in bringing together and facilitating issues which concern a territory larger than the member states, a territory larger than the region. as mr vlasák has already said, it is budgetary neutral in principle. there are so many instruments in regional policy. there has been a separate mountain farmer scheme in rural development for many years. we have instruments, but perhaps we should coordinate them better and facilitate the regions in this respect. so the thinking behind this is: not new structures, but to build on what already exists as much as possible, but to do it in a better way. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council and parliament, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. i hope that the commission will come forward with a policy document soon so that we can discuss the substance further. (the president agreed to answer a 'blue card' question in accordance with rule 149 (8) of the rules of procedure.)
"blue card" is the answer. thank you for asking. it is very important to look at what article 5 says and what the problem is. the question is very simple: how big do you take the territory? how large is the area concerned? it would be wrong at this stage to limit it to the parties to that convention. it should be possible to take into account a larger territory. there's been a lot of debate about that. it is not a question of substance, but of the geographical limitation we are already setting. we don't think that's right. let us, above all, adopt an open approach so that we can have all the instruments that are necessary to achieve the things you are proposing.

the fact that rabies has been virtually eradicated in companion animals does not mean that we should not be vigilant. the problem of stray dogs in the eastern member states in particular is still a major problem. these animals often still have rabies and other diseases. governments in those member states sometimes use cruel means to exterminate these animals. because that 's the way it is . i'm going to kill you. we must continue to call on the eastern european countries to stop these atrocious practices and ensure that they deal properly with these stray dogs. this means that they are caught in an animal-friendly manner, treated for their diseases and castrated or sterilized. it is important that we do not forget these animals when we are adapting this directive.
the introduction of dogs and cats from other member states is a growing phenomenon. my own parents once brought a kitten back from a holiday in spain, a very sweet little thing. however, there are major risks associated with these imports. in some eastern european member states dogs are being bred on a large scale for sale in western european countries. the problem is often the rarer varieties. breeding these dogs is a big business, but it is also a worrying one. accumulating as much money as possible is more important than the health of the dogs. vaccinations against diseases are rare and are kept in appalling conditions. this often leads to the detection of diseases, sometimes long after arrival in the other member state, which can lead to the death of the animal. i would therefore like to call on all member states to be vigilant and to intervene where necessary.

in writing. we are talking today about social housing, and especially about the big cuts in this area. the european commission has pressed for cuts. in the netherlands, rents are rising by up to 6.5% this year, which means hundreds of euros of rent increases per year. people are getting into trouble because of this, and this is the result of eu policies: making social housing inaccessible to middle-income earners, heavily taxing housing corporations, resulting in rising rents and investment freezes: these are all eu proposals that are currently being implemented. the european commission should be ashamed of itself. social housing is an investment in health and well-being and enables people to live independently for longer. the commission should give member states the space and, where necessary, the incentive to provide adequate social housing. investment is also needed to create adequate adapted housing for older people, enabling people to live at home for longer. instead, the commission is pushing for demolition and liberalization and putting tenants in trouble. the delli report condemns the ill-considered policies of the commission and calls for subsidiarity and affordable housing for a wide range of people and deserves our support.
