rapporteur for the opinion of the committee on women’s rights and gender equality. − mr president, i want to urge all colleagues to vote in favour of amendments 71, 72 and 73, which were put forward by alde and the greens and supported by the ecr and epp. these amendments give member states the choice on how much of the fund they want to use and the option of transferring what they do not use to others who need it more. this will provide the highest level of solidarity for three main reasons: firstly, if all member states, including germany and the uk, are forced to participate, the countries worst hit by the crisis will lose millions, leaving people there to starve – even if the fund goes up to eur 3.5 billion. compared with now, there will be a transfer from countries like greece, portugal and spain to countries like germany, the uk, the netherlands and sweden. this is the exact opposite of solidarity. these amendments will prevent this. secondly, while poverty is equally horrible in all member states, it is not the same in all member states. with the amendments, aid organisations will have a choice of measures to help the most deprived in their country best. thirdly, a large blocking minority in council have said that they would rather have no fund at all than an obligatory fund. using this as a negotiating tactic is plain silly. making the fund obligatory will not give them a reason to go up to eur 3.5 billion, it will merely put the whole fund in danger, leaving everybody out in the cold. the amendments will prevent this; please vote in favour of them.
president, according to the european commission, in 2010 many europeans were on the verge of poverty or social exclusion. the majority of this group are citizens of the southern or eastern member states. with a tearful story about 'creating better social cohesion' and other socialist propaganda, the commission wants to spend at least two and a half billion euros on this again. the point is that the northern countries, like the netherlands, can draw their own pockets and the rest of us can make our own money. the commission is not shy about calling the programme's termination an erosion of solidarity. the netherlands has already made a double and total contribution to solidarity. in the netherlands we have good social facilities for people who are unable to provide for themselves. the financial plundering of countries that are doing well must end. mr president, i would like to ask you to withdraw this regulation and not to interfere in this matter.
finally, mr president, we have reached agreement on a comprehensive package on asylum. and although i am certainly granting mrs malmström leave, the real work has yet to begin, because the dublin system is cracking. so there's a major flaw in that system. it is assumed that if you guard your external borders well, you have few asylum requests to process. but that's not true. even if an asylum seeker reports to the border, you must treat the asylum request properly and receive the asylum seeker in a humane manner. the better you guard the borders, the more asylum requests you'll get. if you simply let asylum seekers through in the hope that they will travel clandestinely through your member state to other countries in europe, you will get fewer asylum applications. it is therefore no wonder that the asylum procedure is not in order, particularly in countries with long external borders. asylum seekers are the first victims of this. they cannot even apply for asylum and the conditions of reception are inhuman. for this reason, the european court of justice has prohibited the transfer of asylum applications to certain member states. that puts the ax to the roots of the system. i call on the commission to start a discussion as soon as possible on the design of a new system. that could be a system of european asylum centres, often near the external borders. it also makes asylum a more shared responsibility and allows us all to work together to ensure that asylum applications are dealt with properly. finally, a word about eurodac. the collection and storage of asylum seekers' fingerprints was necessary for dublin to function. however, it was not intended to give the police and the judiciary access to this database. it is criminalising, it is stigmatising and, i say it loud and clear, it is not a crime to apply for asylum. asylum seekers are not automatic suspects. and so my group remains opposed to the eurodac proposal.
"blue card" is the answer. mr president, i was one of those who negotiated eurodac for the netherlands, at least in the initial phase, and i thought it was perfectly normal, difficult but normal for the functioning of the asylum system that you take your fingerprints and that you store them. it's a whole different thing now. what we want now is for the police and the judiciary to be able to consult that database for the purpose of tracing criminals. that is a complete détournement de pouvoir. that is a completely different interpretation of what we intended.
mr president, in a country like the netherlands, it appears that the majority of asylum seekers are not entitled to refugee status. the procedure is therefore designed to distinguish as quickly as possible between genuine refugees and asylum seekers. each member state has years of practical experience of what works and what doesn't work in their country when it comes to identifying those who are likely to qualify for refugee status. the unification of these rules now agreed upon threatens to destroy the experience gained by the member states. the absorptive capacity of societies cannot be ignored in the debate on asylum policy. migration is not just about asylum seekers, but also about family and labour migration. the effects on societies must be seen in terms of total migration. but this debate can only be conducted at the level where its effects are visible, that is, at national level. migration policy must remain national. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them.
mr president, and in particular madam minister, are we now working towards a common european asylum policy, where asylum seekers in every member state have an equal chance of obtaining asylum, where they are properly received, not put in prison, receive legal aid, and where they are given counselling to people who are traumatised? or are we now working towards the point where each member state can fine-tune its policy to be a little more stringent than its neighbours? the dutch state secretary for justice, mr fred teeven, managed to write in the amnesty international newsletter that the intention is that the dutch asylum policy should be a little stricter than that of belgium. if that is the intention, if a member state can get away with honestly saying that it wants to be stricter than its neighbours, there is no common european asylum policy. and that is my conclusion, unfortunately, from what has been presented here.
the agreement on schengen legislation leaves some room for border checks. but the tone is really that border controls no longer belong in the european union. the free movement of persons and goods is sacred, so it sounds. restrictions in the form of border controls should only be allowed in the case of the most extreme exception, for example in the case of a terrorist threat. this line, mr president, is too far for me. the abolition of border controls clearly has a dark side. traffickers have almost free reign within the schengen area. every day, women are being transported across europe and forced into prostitution somewhere. our police services see it happening, but they cannot intervene because the european court of justice has ruled that the dutch marshal cannot use video footage at border posts for more than 90 hours a month. i find that really absurd. the free movement of goods and people is important. and the abolition of border controls has contributed to that free movement. but it is ultimately up to each member state to decide how it wants to maintain public order and combat crime. free movement within europe is a beautiful thing, but let us not be naive. criminals are not. they are happy to take advantage of any opportunity that europe offers them. schengen is ok, but always european open borders, no.
i have opposed schengen on many occasions. the abolition of internal borders may have been a nice idea on paper, but in practice we see that the disadvantages outweigh the benefits many times over. schengen facilitates crime and to combat this we are again coming up with new legislation from brussels, which makes brussels stronger and the member states weaker. the commission has already taken a number of measures to combat this crime, including the introduction of border controls where member states consider it appropriate. what good citizen would not show his passport to prevent criminals and vagrants from invading the host country? nor does passport control in any way affect the principle of free movement of persons. why is everyone so allergic to border controls? schengen is a typical example of a european policy that has gone hopelessly off the rails. i therefore call on the member states once again to choose the interests of their own citizens and to abandon schengen.
president. - after a year of debate on the evaluation mechanism and the possibility of temporarily closing borders, we now have a decision to make. but who's telling who? what's the outcome after a year? member states are allowed to know for themselves and member states are not allowed to show the reasons for their decision to temporarily close a border. they certainly do not want the european commission or the european parliament to be really involved in decision-making. that shows that i think it's mainly intended to tell your own citizens 'oh, we still have the borders in our own hands', when that's not the case elsewhere. the dutch government spends a few hours a month checking the license plates of eastern european vans. they say they're doing it to fight crime. in other words, eastern european license plates indicate dangerous people in dangerous cars, and that's crime-fighting. ladies and gentlemen, the member states are fooling each other and their people.
mr president, we are in a testing phase of the new economic governance. i would like to thank vice-president olli rehn and president barroso for the recommendations to member states. the budgetary objectives take account of the poor economic situation. the recommendations are far-reaching and ambitious in terms of structural reforms. you are right to point out the great risk of an increase in the burden. it is precisely in structural reforms in the member states that an important key lies to economic recovery, to the restoration of competitiveness and to employment growth. i would like to say to our socialist colleagues here: "you don't solve problems by ignoring them". the french president is living proof of that. it is important, mr president, that the heads of government support the recommendations jointly and that each of them show decisiveness. the good examples, however difficult, are the heads of government in ireland, spain, portugal, latvia. and i hope that others, including the netherlands, will follow. but with my spanish colleague, i do say: "europe must do more, and europe must do more". that is why i am concerned about the recent franco-german declaration. the same applies to the banking union. the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the community's financial instruments are used effectively. the commission has also proposed that the commission should be given the task of drawing up a report on the implementation of the reform of the financial sector. and what i want to say in closing is: "commission, come up with an ambitious proposal for the single resolution mechanism. you can count on our support.
in writing. - the compromise now reached between the three european institutions has some good elements. the member states will inform each other when they temporarily introduce border controls and the schengen evaluation mechanism will be removed from the european union. at the same time, the compromise retains a strong community component: the commission's officials are involved in any consultation on border controls and the european parliament must also be informed. on the one hand, the compromise contains good elements which ultimately respect the autonomy of the member states in border control. on the other hand, this compromise is what happens very often in europe: the eu is getting a foot in the door to meddle with border controls. in the long term, this will ensure that the community is able to implement this policy. the netherlands has already noticed this, because the european court of justice in luxembourg has given our country a slap on the wrist in the area of camera surveillance along the borders: these cameras are only allowed to be used for 90 hours a month. that's why i'm against it. i think that the slow but certain communitization of a fundamental right of every state - control over its own borders - is undesirable.
on behalf of the ppe group. – mr president, what started peacefully is now turning into violence. the images have made an impact on all of us, not only in turkey, but in europe and in the world. we in this parliament have always underlined the importance of turkey carrying out reforms in order to safeguard fundamental freedoms. these include the right to protest. it is not only a matter of legal reforms, but also of perception. if the language of prime minister erdoğan’s comments had been more sensitive, and if his style of governing had been more sensitive to those who did not vote for him, this situation would not have arisen. therefore i totally agree with president gül that democracy is about more than elections. a majority always needs to consider the position of the minority. today mr erdoğan is meeting the taksim platform and i sincerely hope that he will use this opportunity to present himself as leader of all citizens. however, a vibrant democracy also needs a competent, constructive opposition, and that also [is lacking]. i believe that there are, at least, challenges for the opposition. this parliament has consistently criticised the situation as regards media freedom in turkey. i think that the past two weeks have confirmed our concerns. i hope that the recent events, devastating as they are, might prove to be a turning point for turkey.
president, last month mr van rompuy, who was lyrical about the so-called progress in turkey, thought it a good idea to speed up the accession talks with turkey. he even invited prime minister erdogan to visit brussels. a miscalculation of epic proportions! and that's not wisdom in hindsight. the demonstrations in turkey may seem to have come relatively suddenly, but the same cannot be said of the islamization agenda and repression of the erdogan regime. this has been warned by many, including the freedom party. but the european union is blind to reality. the european union has been a major player in the process of democratization in turkey. in an earlier statement, mrs ashton said the eu was determined to resume dialogue with turkey. erdogan is going to go on a rampage. and what does the eu do? he's just gonna roll the red carpet clean for erdogan. mr president, this is absurd! enough is enough! as far as the pvv is concerned, erdogan is not welcome in brussels at all! the eu is not worth a nickel if it sends this islamist another invitation!
president. - the turkish minister for european affairs yesterday called his prime minister, mr erdogan, a gift from god to the nation. this political evaluation is tantamount to the acute lack of realism displayed by the lauded prime minister erdogan himself in these tumultuous taksimids. worse still, the public discourse of the turkish prime minister is characterized by deliberate social polarization. sadly, erdogan continues to heat up the public atmosphere by blaming outright untruths on the behavior of protesters at taksim square in istanbul. take, for example, the debunked fabrication about the desecration of a mosque. more controversially, however, i find the comparison made by the prime minister in a long speech yesterday between the largely peaceful protesters and the perpetrators of the murderous attack in the south-east of the country earlier last month. and that's against the backdrop of brutal police brutality against his critics. the council and the commission must not allow this complete lack of political self-criticism and sense of responsibility to go unnoticed! can you give me a concrete example? because at the end of last week, prime minister erdogan did not even bother to hear commissioner füle's interpretation. the commission has already made a number of proposals to this effect. the economic power derived from a decade of akp dominance certainly deserves close european attention. the turkish government's cash-crane over 40 billion euros a year in public procurement virtually silences both the public and private media on serious corruption cases. the controversial editor-in-chief of cnn türk admits it to the dutch press: "the protesters don't realize that we don't make the decisions. these are our bosses who have interests in all sectors of the economy. our coverage must not interfere with those interests. so we don't report on corruption". sure, turkey has plenty of critical media, thankfully. in 2006 alone, this led to the discovery of no less than 271 cases of acp manipulation of public procurement. the point is, however, that the main culprit not only got away with it, but is now even part of the erdogan cabinet! and next week he's coming to brussels! the akp should be cleansing itself of its own corruption, especially after the massive nationwide protests of the past few weeks. and not to the exclusion of the party, as the responsible akp parliamentarian from hatay province felt. honesty is the longest lasting virtue. it is also for turkish society. in this spirit of openness i am also looking forward to the delegation talks next week on the territory of the republic of turkey.
mr president, via social media we have seen many people: men, women, pious, secular, young, old, bankers, entrepreneurs, students; they all want their voice to be heard. they have been largely peaceful: pots and pans have been used to voice concern. the people in turkey are experiencing the dictatorship of a majority, their voices unheard as a result of restrictions in media freedom, the high threshold for political parties and the government’s winner-takes-all mentality. the weakness of the opposition surely does not help either. but the government, which presents itself as full of ambition and confidence internationally, must act responsibly. they must urge restraint in police violence and see dialogues and reforms. this does not mean arresting lawyers, fining the media and inciting people. personal threats by the president, the prime minister and the akp party towards the media, social media, financial institutions and all of the citizens that they claim to represent is worrying, dangerous and misguided. let us be honest, as honest as turkish politicians tend to be towards us. we have addressed the problems with the rule of law, separation of powers, fundamental rights and the need to respect the rights of women and the media. once more we would like to stress the need for actual democratisation, beyond ‘one man, one vote’. turkey needs to respect pluralism and freedoms and strive to be a liberal democracy, not an illiberal one.
mr president, the protestors today deserve the same solidarity as the girls with headscarves who were peacefully demonstrating for their rights some years ago. the feeling deep down is the same; the reaction is against governments imposing their preferred lifestyles on the citizens. we need pluralism and democracy for everyone. the people in the streets of turkey are of various backgrounds, ages and beliefs. we see the devout muslims praying; others doing yoga; turkish nationalist and kurdish activists, lgbt groups and rival football supporters side by side. the government and the opposition have a lot to learn from them. as for the violence, this excessive force by police should immediately come to an end. there should be an immediate end to the hostile rhetoric. the authorities should do their utmost to find an inclusive and peaceful solution to the situation. however, the actions of the authorities do not seem to improve. yesterday over 50 lawyers were taken into custody for protesting in the courthouse in favour of the peaceful demonstrators. the events of the last days remind us of the importance of the role of the eu. negotiations in chapters 23 and 24 need to be opened. the turkish government wants this; the demonstrators want this, since these are the chapters where prevention of torture and ill-treatment, impunity, freedom of expression and assembly, the media and police cooperation are dealt with. therefore, there seems to be no obstacle to making the necessary reforms. these issues are currently being covered by the positive agenda but since the current developments are not very positive these chapters need to be opened.
rapporteur. − madam president, madam high representative, special representative, colleagues, i am glad we are here together to discuss this important topic. in syria, over 150 journalists have been murdered over the past two and a half years. the violence makes it nearly impossible to report independently on the violence and the war. only this week in turkey, which has more journalists in prison than any other country in the world, the president stated that social media are more dangerous than a bomb and regulators fined media for ‘inciting hatred’. these actions speak volumes about the power of the free word. in iran, in the run-up to the elections, the media and internet have been restricted even more than we deemed possible. in russia, the government has granted itself authority to use deep packet inspections of the internet. journalists and bloggers have been murdered and beaten up. intimidation is hardly investigated. in china, an estimated 30 000 people work on monitoring micro-blogging sites and adjusting the terms of censorship, some of which have been set technologically to make sure no search results are found when looking for information on, for example, tiananmen square. these countries are not at all unique in their repression of media and journalists. there are many countries where governments are using anti-terrorism and other national security laws to repress. other problems are political interference through public media budgets, tax fines, or when politicians are owners of ever-growing media conglomerates. of course media are still used for outright propaganda as well. the criminalisation of speech, such as through blasphemy laws, libel and restricting ‘homosexual propaganda’, is also worrying. the eu as a community of values should aspire to lead in ensuring that the free word, whether blogged or spoken, and information, whether research or photographs, are protected. journalists and free pluralist media are essential for checks on power; and a focus on neighbouring countries is natural. by improving coordination between the directorate-general for development cooperation (devco) and the external action service, we can achieve stronger results and impacts globally. we must mainstream press freedom in all our policies on election observations, democracy, neighbourhood policy, trade and development programmes. the programmes we have should be deployed more effectively, but new ones are also necessary. journalists facing trial should be able to rely on our know-how and on a legal defence fund. the end goal in all of our programmes should be that people do not depend on eu funding but that programmes can run independently, that local capacity is built to stay. we must engage both at government level and at local and civil society levels. indeed the private sector also has a role to play to foster open societies. we call on businesses to take their responsibility. with growing digitisation, the public value of information must be preserved. self-regulation and privatising of policing and law enforcement on line are dangerous. at the same time we see opportunities in the empowerment of individuals who, through social media, can access information, assemble and speak freely. citizen journalism makes us eye-witnesses to events as they unfold. in order to ensure our own independence in this parliament and to make sure that the rankings of countries are not politicised, we have not mentioned countries explicitly in this report but instead we have addressed trends and problems faced and we have focused on concrete solutions. but the assessment on the country-by-country situation is left to the combined work of four ngos and the work of them you will find in the annexes. it will be updated on a rolling basis and i want to thank them for their work, as well as the shadow rapporteurs. we have worked very well as a team and it has been great to receive input from stakeholders, journalists, bloggers and people all over the world on an online draft that has certainly made the work of this report and the content of this report more relevant on the ground. it is closely related to the digital freedom strategy for the eu’s foreign policy which was adopted last december and we look forward to hearing about how a strategy for press and media freedom will be set up by the european external action service and the commission. i believe that we have both the responsibility and an opportunity that we can take together.
president, religions and ideologies are the ultimate evil of this world. centuries-old dogmas are being forced upon people and children to impose backward, outdated and idiotic worldviews. religions make people stupid and keep people stupid, despite the scientific truth that there are no supernatural beings. the right of religions to exist is that they impose rules of life which contain a self-proclaimed truth. rules of conduct that are considered superior to empirically proven facts. everyone has the right to think in his head whatever he wants, however crazy it may be and however detached it may be from any sense of reality. human rights are based on the need to protect those who are discriminated against on the basis of something that is beyond their own free will, something they are born with or something that is done to them. think of having a certain skin color, disability, race, sexual orientation or gender. these people deserve protection from any form of arbitrariness. and i'm with you on that. but believing in a deus is a choice. it's not congenital. it's a choice people make with their heads full of wisdom - or so you'd think. sadly, children are brainwashed from an early age to adopt the beliefs of their environment. it's your most childish abuse, because no one is born believing, you are made to. believers must always be able to be ridiculed and mocked, to be ridiculed and constantly called upon for their delusions. blasphemy is the nail in the coffin of free speech and common sense. hurting and insulting believers is a right that everyone has. because, if you want to believe in god, allah, buddha or whatever, you have to accept that you're going against all forms of human logic and that there's a price to that in a free democratic society, which is that you can be ridiculed for your idiotic ideas. and you're just gonna have to accept that. i'm going round. believing is something you do at home, not in public. it should be a human right not to be confronted with age-old dogmas and to be allowed to ridicule them.
blue-card question. – i listened to you, colleague, and i have just one question: would you think these are appropriate methods, to put people out from one day to the next and to close down television stations, just like that, as if we were speaking about a coup d’état? i would say these are methods that are neither accepted by the social charter of the european union, nor by a score of ilo conventions, and i am amazed that you would just accept them like that.
mr president, rightly this annual report of the european external action service pays a great deal of attention to religious freedom. i have three comments to make. first, the report shows that religious freedom is under pressure. millions of people in many, many countries of the world are being persecuted for their faith, and the problem is now so vast that fat reports or fat statements no longer work. it is now time for the high representative to take action and use instruments such as trade policy and development aid to really fight this oppression. that's one. two of them. the foreign office currently lacks a department that is genuinely concerned with religious freedom. my call is: the commission is therefore proposing to set up a department which can also monitor developments and take concrete action. finally, the european parliament is not silent either, you know that president we now have a working group in the house which is focusing on religious freedom. i'm allowed to co-chair that. i say again to the high representative: we are happy to work with you to develop that issue of religious freedom.
mr president, freedom of expression is a fundamental human right. it is ‘the touchstone of all the freedoms to which the united nations is consecrated’ – that is how the un formulated the importance of freedom of expression in 1946, at its very first session. more than 60 years later there are still countries where journalism is the most dangerous of all professions. indeed, for journalists, 2012 was the deadliest year ever registered, with somalia, syria, mexico and pakistan as the most risky countries. freedom of information simply does not exist in countries like eritrea, north korea and turkmenistan. in the case of some countries, where our strategic interests come into play, the eu and the usa show a guilty silence on the lack of respect for human rights. turkmenistan is such a country, as it is important for us for our access to afghanistan. this is appalling. turkmen citizens are waiting for europe to assume its responsibility and condemn clearly the violation in turkmenistan of freedoms we are happy to enjoy here. the added value of the eu as an actor in the world is its potential political weight: it is stronger than the member states could ever be individually. so let us show this added value, let us help and defend imprisoned journalists, let us defend the freedom of the internet and hold governments to account. the eu could also do better in assisting the media in developing countries. journalists are essential in the struggle against corruption, and in striving towards better governance that takes better account of people’s needs, particularly those of the most vulnerable. this excellent report by my colleague ms schaake shows the way. in our negotiations on the future development policy of the eu, freedom of expression is an essential aspect of the human-rights-based approach that the eu should have at the heart of its external action.
madam president, i salute the commitment of the vice-president/high representative and our commissioner for development, and i strongly share their vision. but after thousands of soldiers have been killed, after hundred of billions of euros have been spent in afghanistan – more than in any developing country – many donors in the international community want to leave as fast as possible. this is understandable, but also wrong. in 2014, the few clear results reached in the country will still be very fragile. girls going to school, and an administration – albeit one plagued by corruption – that is gradually improving its functioning, are improvements that can erode very quickly if we stop investing in development and in governance, as my colleague mr danjean has just said, and if we stop training the army and the police force. the first victims will be women, and already violence against women is on the rise again, after a decade of improvements. we have to continue and strengthen our presence with the eupol training mission, as the police still lack the skills to act properly when women are confronted with violence, most often domestic violence. taliban insurgents are spreading fear, and children are now asking their mothers to stop working in order to stay alive. the afghan constitution needs to be respected in the peace negotiations. such is the wish of the vast majority of the afghans. therefore, we, the eu, will need to continue our efforts, as commissioner piebalgs has just said, for many years to come.
rapporteur for the opinion of the committee on development and cooperation. - i have indeed proposed to the committee on development cooperation that the committee on international trade should advise the committee on economic and monetary affairs not to ratify this interim epa. i didn't make it. the committee on development and cooperation proposes that it be ratified. but now when i listen to david martin explaining why this interim epa is not perfect, when i hear mr piebalgs saying that it is not working enough towards regional trade, i think everyone knows exactly why this is not good for cameroon, but for lack of a better one it should be. we are following a circular line, we are turning ourselves into a circle. because why should it? because, of course, cameroon must be able to continue to market its products in europe at a better rate. but why is that again? because we ourselves have recently changed that market exit regulation to actually make it a little bit more difficult for developing countries. we in europe continue to force developing countries which need a different trade policy to sign free trade agreements, which we now call economic partnership agreements. it sounds like development cooperation, but it is ultimately free trade for our products, and there is not enough consideration given to what it means to develop a country in africa or the caribbean. and that makes me very sad.
today we are discussing the millennium development goals for post-2015. as if europe had no more important things to discuss in the midst of this enormous economic crisis we are experiencing! a crisis that has come about, in particular, thanks to the misguided policies of this eu, but that is beside the point. i am here to advocate the fight against forced marriage of girls, the gender gap in education, violence and discrimination against women and girls in general and the prevention of sexual violence in particular. a fine case in itself, but i miss the point of naming the root cause of all these problems, namely islam. the problem is almost exclusively one of muslim-dominated countries, but it is obviously not a matter to be discussed here in parliament. a stunning political correctness, which is life threatening, especially for the women and girls they claim to be protecting. as long as this parliament does not want to identify the cause, there can be no solution. i can be brief about all the nonsense that is being said about climate, the environment and sustainability. this is crazy! no more spending a penny! wisdom, understanding and truth were conspicuously absent in the writing of this account. i 'll say it again . it's too dangerous! the ppe group will therefore vote against this report.
madam president, the most important message of this excellent report by filip kaczmarek is that trade and the market alone will not bring an end to poverty. some would like to try to revive this liberal utopia in the developing world, although it found its waterloo in the financial crisis here. of course we have to facilitate trade – fair trade, sustainable trade. we have to ensure access for all to loans, savings, micro-finance and more, that will allow people to improve their livelihoods. financial inclusion is a condition for inclusive growth. we can end poverty by 2030, but not without accountable governments addressing the needs of the poorest and most vulnerable in their countries: of minorities, women and farmers. if the poorest are not heard in their capitals, only the elites will enjoy an unstable wellbeing and that is what makes the human rights-based approach of the eu so crucially important. development is not a technocratic problem – some fertiliser here, some market there – it is about assisting people in changing their societies if they so wish, step by step, reaching a gradual enlargement of freedoms.
in writing. − the sgp group supports the development of eu guidelines for the promotion and protection of freedom of religion or belief. the draft recommendation by rapporteur andrikiene contains valuable elements to this end. the right to change one's religion and the right to freedom of christian education are both being put on the map again. that is why i am voting in favour of this report. the european parliament has also called for the commission to be given the task of drawing up a report on the situation in the baltic sea region. this does not do any justice to the disparity between the christian religion and the non-christian beliefs mentioned. this objection is also clearly expressed by the secular opponents of the andrikiene report. their fierce opposition was an additional incentive for the sgp euro group to support this report despite the objections mentioned.

in addition to numerous amendments to the constitution, a new law on national security has been adopted in hungary. under the new national security law, diplomats, judges, prosecutors, and high-ranking military personnel must be re-tested every year to determine whether or not they pose a national security risk. to test this, they are spied on for up to two months each year, even in their homes, perhaps even in their bedrooms. they just don't know which two months out of the 12 months the government is watching them and they don't know what the government is looking for. in the meantime, we are angry, surprised and outraged from left to right in this parliament at the spying practices of the united states on our citizens, our diplomats, our missions. how is it then that the right in this house thinks that the orbán government has the right to do whatever it wants with its citizens and its dignitaries in its own country? citizens will feel insecure, citizens will never know, when they wake up in the morning, what the rule of law will look like now! franz kafka couldn't have made this up. one writer, however, did, namely george orwell, in his book "1984".
mr president, it is a shame that this debate is being turned into a left-right issue. fundamental rights are not left-wing; they are not right-wing; they are fundamental. they are for everybody. they do not belong to any particular nationality either. i have to say that i am always quite surprised to see the position of the epp group. there are some very sensible, real democrats in the epp group, and i have heard some sensible words as well. at the same time, it strikes me that you always seem to be defending the wrong people. you used to defend silvio berlusconi. now you are defending mr orbán. we are talking about friends of hungary. friends, mr orbán, dare to tell each other the truth. and if we see that things are not going well, we should have the courage to tell each other. that is real friendship. i do not think that this debate should be about how we fit wrong policies, questionable policies, into legalistic definitions – this is about creating a true culture of fundamental rights for all eu citizens. finally, we are often reproached for applying double standards. there is some truth in that. yes, maybe in the past we were not vigilant enough, maybe in the past we did not consider that we should speak out, but we are learning. it is a learning process and we have criticised fundamental rights violations in other countries too.
mr. president, the transaction tax is a bad idea. when sweden introduced a transaction tax in 1984, over 30% of the trade volume moved to britain and japan also stopped for this reason. the transaction tax was announced here as a tax on the banks that are starting out. but does it hit the benches? it's hitting the consumer and the economy. a transaction fee increases the cost of investing, reduces trading volume and increases volatility. the introduction of this transaction tax through enhanced cooperation is also totally illegal. the treaty is quite clear on this point. if there are effects on the internal market, a measure may not be introduced in the context of enhanced cooperation. and the transaction tax has effects on the internal market. this shows that both the commission and parliament, which rejected an amendment of mine on this subject, are guilty of deception and abuse of power. it's a bad idea, it's an illegal idea, and we're going to implement it anyway. that, mr president, is brussels in a nutshell.
on behalf of the ppe group i too would like to begin by thanking the rapporteur for her efforts. the epp initially had problems with your somewhat one-sided british approach. but the report as it has been amended respects the diversity of the european banking landscape and at the same time seeks to restrict 'risky speculation on its own account'. the epp considers it important that a european framework for the structure of banks should be in line with the many reforms already under way: the commission has also proposed to increase the capital requirements, ecb banking supervision, a bonus cap and crisis management rules. a number of member states are working on their own proposals for the division of banks, but a patchwork of legislation does not fit into a banking union. that is why it is important that you, commissioner barnier, intend to come forward with a european legislative proposal. i am not saying that we should not be concerned about the future of the community. the epp considers it important that the social function of banks should be given priority and that the universal banking model should be preserved. banks should also be able to continue to carry out customer-related trading activities, thereby providing loans to smes and businesses. that's part of it, part of that important oxygen, because loans are essential for economic growth. speculative activities which have nothing to do with customers must be separated because they have brought unnecessary risks to universal banks.
it is regrettable, also in the light of the last remarks by mr geier, that the council is not present this evening. first of all, my compliments to ingeborg gräßle. she has done her job with the usual care. a few months ago we thought the result would be good. in the meantime, a number of other events have taken place, including with commissioner dalli, and she has found it necessary to table a number of amendments. these amendments - and i told her that - are not bad at all. the only problem for me is what happens if we take them? will the council really stop negotiating? perhaps the commissioner can shed some light on this. however, a clear problem is the relationship between the supervisory committee and olaf itself. i would therefore like to propose the following and i would like to hear the commissioner's opinion on this : would it be useful to appoint a committee of three or four members at short notice, with access to olaf and to give an opinion to parliament/commission, in order to improve the relationship between the monitoring committee and olaf?
i would like to say that i am very pleased that the commission has taken this initiative. the situation at olaf is serious. first, the conflict with the monitoring committee will have to be resolved. some of our proposals are designed to do that. it is also worth recommending, as mr mulder said, the setting up of a 'committee of the wise' to bring about a lasting improvement in the situation. secondly, the low number of convictions in cases investigated by olaf is interpreted by the commission as a failure of member states' policies. but is it not also the quality of olaf's own work? i am getting these signals from the member states and i would like to see an independent, external investigation into this. finally, we must get rid of the pressure which the commission puts on olaf to do things in a hurry, and therefore often in a sloppy way. i have spoken to mr kessler about this and he does not like the pressure either. there is still a lot of work to be done, and that is why i do not think it is a disaster, but rather an opportunity, if the adoption of the regulation is delayed for a few months.
mrs gräßle has examined the functioning of olaf. that is a good thing, because olaf is not functioning. this is very serious, because the commission and parliament claim to be fighting fraud. but if the anti-fraud office is no longer in operation, it will be very difficult to take that claim seriously. i know this from my own experience after reporting a fraud case in a country far, far away. although olaf thought it worthwhile to have a full investigation, i have not heard anything more from the investigation, even as an informer. so this is very serious. there are hundreds of cases waiting in olaf's offices, but i have no doubt that they will be dealt with properly. so today we should not be talking about better olaf or other ways of working, but about putting an end to the eu circus here. that saves a huge amount of money and automatically and omnipotently prevents a lot of fraud.
mr president, first i would like to congratulate the president-in-office of the council on the first lithuanian presidency. with great delight, i heard her address issues such as european values, the year of the european citizen, and say that she wanted people to be able to access their liberties and release their potential. she said that she wanted lithuania to lead by example. i would very much encourage the lithuanian presidency to start leading by example by lifting the ban on the pride march in the centre of vilnius. when i go to vilnius at the end of this month, i want to see vilnius celebrating diversity. i want to see a city which is celebrating openness and tolerance, which is an image of a city that is very colourful and embraces everybody. i think you will agree with me, madam president-in-office, that homophobia is not the answer to these uncertain times. homophobia does not create jobs and is certainly not very good city marketing. this afternoon there will be another court ruling and i hope it is favourable. i hope to see you in vilnius on 27 july and celebrate diversity in europe.
mr president, 2014 is an important year to initiate the flow of funds that we created in the mff, and to make investments a reality. for the european structural and investment funds, we have a big budget of eur 325 billion available until 2020, and at the same time we see that a maximum core of 26.5 million people are unemployed. the core point is: if there are more people at work we can do it in a smarter and greener way. we have to negotiate with the council, with you president-in-office, to get agreement on three other issues. firstly, the market of conditionality: parliament is against the possibility of putting money from the regions aside during the period. secondly, the performance reserve: there is also a proposal to put eur 30 billion aside during the next seven years and maybe we can only spend it after 2020. thirdly, the border regions: they are the real victims from a financial perspective. taken together we have to negotiate on these important points. i have a lot of trust in your presidency. you did a great job in your own country, and so let us get down to work and create jobs.
i do not want to say at this point that the irish presidency was the worst presidency of all time, because there were others before that who did a lot less well, but it was not all pretty. we are going to be stuck with a multiannual financial framework which is completely unclear how it will work in the coming years. the only constant in the agreements is the fact that we will see money disappear from the northern member states to the southern ones. fortunately, each member state still has a veto on these. let us hope that at least one member state will dare to take the step of throwing this agreement into the trash.
president. - the next item is the report (a4-0173/97) by mr de gucht, on behalf of the committee on economic and monetary affairs and industrial policy, on the proposal from the commission to the council (com (97) 0173 - c4-0173/97). unfortunately, the climate atoll has succeeded today. if you don't like the outcome of a carbon trading market, then you shouldn't make a market out of it. that is a very simple reasoning. apart from that, of course, i cannot repeat often enough that it is of course an illusion that with a working co2 regulatory system we can stop or reverse the supposed climate change and save the world. what a shocking naivete of the climate fundamentalists.
mr president, just like the trading of co2 emission allowances, the financial transaction tax is also mentioned many times. the netherlands rightly opposes this disguised source of income for the money-spinning eu. the ftt, of course, was created in response to the mess that the banks made of it, not ordinary citizens. unfortunately, it does not punish the banks, it does not provide additional stability and the burden is simply paid by the citizen. it is extremely wrong to add to the burden of those citizens, especially at this time. the ftt is a mixture of european maladministration and economic populism.
the report on hungary is perhaps the most unstable project i have seen pass by here in the last four years. how can parliament think of insulting and mocking a free and sovereign country in such a way? what's next? inquisitors from the ep are travelling through europe looking for citizens who do not know the european anthem or who refuse to bow to the european flag. mr president, there are only three words that apply to this report: shame, shame, shame on you. i say to the voters in this house: please mind your own business!
in writing. - i shall abstain on this report. on the one hand, it is clear to me that hungary has gone too far in limiting the powers of the judiciary, the central bank and the media. the independence of the judiciary and the bank has been undermined and there is certainly cause for concern about the pluriformity of the media. on the other hand, the report has become a disjointed tangle of numerous opinions and views. the debate is completely polarized. but most of all: the report's present course is too one-sided. in a sense, a crusade is being launched against the autonomous member state of hungary. and who knows, tomorrow it will be the same for other member states. europe is not going to be able to do that and will sit in the seat of a moral judge. a chair that does not belong to europe in this scale and intensity.
in writing. - i cannot support mr mccarthy's report. the report suggests a breakdown of banks into customer-related credit and deposit activities and risky trading and investment activities. splitting is a controversial measure, however. both bankers and central bankers have serious reservations. for example, how well are both parts funded separately? the commission has already taken a number of measures to ensure that the community's financial resources are used to the full. on the other hand, the report rightly calls attention to the culture within the banking sector where a responsible remuneration system is geared to long-term financial stability rather than short-term profits. the cultural and moral aspect includes serving the interests of customers and the social role of banks vis-à-vis consumers, businesses and the economy as a whole.
on behalf of the alde group. – mr president, i have to say i am a bit disappointed by the statements of the council and the commission although i know that commissioner reding made huge efforts. however, they do not really seem to reflect the urgency and the magnitude of the problem. it struck me, ms reding, that you referred first to international relations and then to citizens’ rights and i think that accurately reflects the attitude of the commission and the council in this matter. we have asked the commission, in particular, and the council for years and years and years to clarify the conflict of jurisdiction. we need somebody to tell the united states or indeed any other country: this is where your jurisdiction reaches; this is where the european jurisdiction starts. we can no longer tolerate that us laws like the patriot act and fisa are applied directly on eu territory. colleagues, we are legislators; we make laws and we have to guarantee to our citizens that they are covered by european law and not american law. i do not want to hear the argument of national security anymore. it is not credible any more. blue-card question. – my colleague had just said – and this is often assumed – that this massive data collection is very effective in, as you said, thwarting terrorist attacks. however, what do you say then about the fact that for years there has been this massive data collection and yet in attacks such as 9/11, mumbai, oslo, toulouse, madrid, london, the dutch killing of theo van gogh and the ‘doner killings’ in germany all the perpetrators were known to the secret services? so how effective is it really? if these people were known, if we had so much information, how was it possible that they were not detected?

on behalf of the alde group. – mr president, as we stand here, the egyptian people are flooding the streets in the largest political demonstrations ever seen. the situation is very tense. the government should not pit people against each other. violent clashes, and especially a civil war, must be prevented. though president morsi – or any government following in the corrupt footsteps of the mubarak dictatorship – would have had major challenges to reform the economy and to guide the country towards democracy, power grabs and restricting the rights of minorities, limiting and criminalising civil society and failing to avoid a bread and fuel crisis have culminated in an untenable situation. morsi has lost the legitimacy he once had in the eyes of many egyptians. it is encouraging to see so many egyptians reject the dictatorship of the majority, seeking the rule of law and the protection of the rights, liberties and opportunities of all egyptians. democracy is more than one man, one vote. the eu, too, must get its act together. so far ‘more for more’ rings hollow, as a hollow promise. we must not repeat the mistakes of the past. we must reach out to the population of egypt more forcefully and more strongly to help, in the immediate term, to prevent violence and to help facilitate an inclusive dialogue towards transition and free and fair elections, towards a rules-based society and a new constitution and towards economic recovery and necessary reforms, while respecting the rights and freedoms of all egyptians. we must do what we can to offer a democratic perspective to a massive young generation of egyptians who need such perspectives and look to europe. we cannot fail them.
i am pleased to note that the commission has taken the initiative of inviting mr morsi to the house immediately after his inauguration. unfortunately, that has not yet happened and, given the situation in the last few days, i do not think it will happen. that is a great pity, because we could have had a good debate with him, perhaps something like the one we had yesterday with prime minister orbán of hungary. morsi has not shown his best democratic side. he has given himself extra powers, he is sticking to a very restrictive ngo law, religious freedom is being curtailed and parliamentary elections have been postponed until further notice. we were in cairo for the eu-egypt task force. we have solid funds ready to help egypt with its economy and its democracy, and again, it is not the time to release those funds. they'll still be available. but what should be our response? disapprove of what's happening in cairo now? pressuring for new elections for parliament and perhaps for the president? we have to stick to it, because the salafists were the second party in egypt in the elections. where are those disappointed morsi voters going? it sometimes seems as if this house thinks that they would all sign the membership card of the liberal international. but there is a chance that we will infuse this country with a sea of conservative thinking that will not do the position of free thinkers, of women, of minorities any good. anyone who has been saying in the past days and in the past debates 'let's take our hands off egypt, let's keep our pennies, let's keep putting conditions' (which we have been putting for a long time) should understand that this country must be brought towards a healthy democracy. because one thing we know for sure: in a democracy, a president is not elected by an army.
for the epp group when i called cairo this morning with my regular informant, he told me honestly that upon waking up his first thought was to go quickly to see how many deaths had occurred last night in cairo where he has lived for years. now, less than an hour ago, i had him on the line again, and another friend in the arab world who said he was a little shocked and concerned that there is no public order at all in mass demonstrations at the moment. the police are not present, understandably because of the unpopularity, but the army is also absent. that filled them and me with great fear, actually. in view of the complexity of the situation, the following questions arise: with all the justified criticism you can have of president morsi and his eventual forced departure it's all very much the question is there not a dangerous precedent where 'the street' (because that is the people) demands and governs? the egyptian opposition, which is deeply divided, has no programme to save the country from the economic abyss into which egypt is inexorably sinking. furthermore, a victory of the liberal and secular parties in early elections is highly unlikely, if one looks at the recent opinion polls. many of them will be radical, anti-western, salafist parties. after all, is the egyptian army our last hope to prevent total chaos and a bloody civil war? that would be a pretty big sign, if you look at egypt's recent past. not a very encouraging prospect.
mr president, in the netherlands we have a nice saying: 'don't let the cheese eat the bread'. sorry to interrupt so late at night, but it means something like "stand your ground" in english. that is exactly what we must do as the eu towards norway, to stand our ground, not to let us eat the cheese from the bread. because through various trade agreements norway has undertaken to open up markets, including for agricultural products. the skyrocketing taxes we are talking about today simply do not fit that. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. the european commission may say 'yes, that effect is not so great, because it is not our largest trading partner', but for an individual horticulturist in my region that simply means the end of his business. it's as simple as that. one thing is simple: if norway is to benefit from access to the european market, europe must also have access to the norwegian market. these norwegian trade barriers have been in place for several months now and i see very little concrete action by the european commission so far. i think that "doing nothing" sends a very wrong signal, also for the future. we therefore call on the european commission to increase pressure on norway to start concrete talks at high level on the removal of the obstacles. if norway does not cooperate, countermeasures will be taken. the example of norwegian salmon is obvious. of course, always with the aim of returning to the normal relations that should exist between the european union and our good friend norway. but until then, we will not be eating the cheese off the bread. we will give that signal tomorrow as a parliament and we call on the commission to cooperate.
mr president, the single market is both one of the eu’s strongest legacies and one its most prevailing myths. it is not finished. when it comes to the digital single market, certainly we must move mountains of barriers to unlock the hundreds of billions of euros in value that we can add to the eu’s economy. the priorities for the mff stand in stark contrast to what is needed in order to make the eu the world’s most competitive digital market. that should be our ambition, but it requires resources for the next century – not the last. we have also been eagerly awaiting substantial proposals by the commission to reform and harmonise intellectual property rights. the current, fragmented system that looks at copyright through the lens of the printing press in a time of hyper-connectivity is not relevant any more. the reforms must reflect the public interest of access to knowledge, culture and information and the need to stop disproportionately protecting incumbents in the market. we need innovation and competition. that is why it is also high time that the commission enshrines net neutrality in law. we must ensure a level playing field online and should respect and protect the open internet. citizens expect more from governments, and rightfully so. open data, open standards, e-government and ict skills in education must all contribute to better delivery for our people.
the report on access to care for vulnerable groups, mr president, calls for the eu to intervene in the health care policies of the member states. the rapporteur believes that the european semester should also take into account issues such as accessibility and quality of care services. it doesn't get much crazier! the report even advocates free health care for vulnerable populations. mr president, there is no such thing as free, because someone has to pay the bill. this report is yet another example of the terrible euro-philist urge for comprehensive eu involvement. in the netherlands we have excellent social and medical facilities that are accessible to every citizen. the european union's intervention will only allow countries that are doing their job to pull the strings to help countries that are not doing their job. the socialist eu policy has repeatedly rewarded inefficiency and lack of organisation. mr president, let it be clear that health and social security policy are national affairs. and let it be made even clearer that this must remain the case with the pvv!
mrs lambert makes very good suggestions to ensure care for vulnerable groups and i support her. but we must not ignore the real cause of the fact that more and more people have no money for the doctor. the rapporteur rightly calls the elderly a vulnerable group. the elderly become vulnerable when their homes are being saved up, when the aow is being cut and when your pension is put at risk by illegitimate measures of the european union. but other groups are also becoming vulnerable. raise people's wages, take away their permanent employment contracts, make them travel long distances for medical treatment, yes, that makes people vulnerable. brussels is deliberately pushing this. it is therefore naive to ask the european commission to make recommendations to protect these vulnerable groups. the commission itself has caused these consequences. it is up to the eu to step back from this crisis and give member states financial space, especially when it comes to basic needs such as healthcare.

together with the wider unrest in the sahel region, could lead to a spillover and a worsening situation.

- a writer. − at the beginning of the year, on one of the coldest days, i was walking with a party member of mine in almere. for those of you who don't know, almere is in the netherlands in a polder, land made by the dutch, and it's a city that has yet to grow. it is a city that lies where a lake once was and part of that city is built by the people themselves. it's been cleared, people can build their own houses. what energy was released! if only we could, say, just tap into that light! because people were all saying, i'm going to build the most energy-efficient house, i'm going to build an energy-producing house. there was a tremendous amount of creativity that was unleashed, and that creativity has remained untapped until now. unused in the rest of the netherlands and unused in the rest of europe. we just see that there's enough to tap into. europe, as we have just seen in the previous debate, faces many energy challenges: i would like to thank the commissioner for his report, which is a very good report, and i hope that the commission will be able to support it. the european parliament and the european commission - and i commend you for that - are also working hard on a post-2020 energy policy: the commission has also proposed that the council should adopt a decision on the renewal of the community's nuclear programme. but you and the commission have one very big blind spot. i have raised this matter with you several times over the years. do you have an eye for small? because we did great things in the past, we want to do great things in the future, because it's very difficult to imagine the future differently from the past that you've known. when we think of energy, we think of big. big coal plants, so big sustainability plants. so we all love desertec, a big project in the desert. we like big offshore wind turbines and big works, big infrastructure. but that's a very classic way. that's not disruptive thinking, just seeing something that wasn't there. so stop looking at the big picture. i am not saying that we should stop, but that the real innovation, the real revolution in energy is in the small. small is beautiful. give power, give power to that little consumer. turn him from a consumer into a prosumer. that's small-scale energy. we must provide more information and raise awareness about this. but we do see that once consumers are allowed to produce their own energy, they themselves go to extremes and that a great deal of innovative power is also unleashed. it must be possible to produce this small-scale energy independently of the large energy companies. how does the consumer become a prosumer? so power to the people, because they're very creative. and it can also save money for the consumer. we talk about energy efficiency all the time. how can you be more efficient than if a consumer generates his own energy and thereby becomes aware of his own energy behaviour? but there are many, many barriers. there is too little information about the potential, whereas studies show that by 2050 30% of the uk's energy could come from small-scale energy. what does this mean for the european union? have you ever looked into that? the technology is already there, and so is the consumer. but if the policy and the governments step in, will the european union step in? the investment price is still too high and we see that there are all kinds of obstacles in national legislation, as well as european tax legislation, which prevent all of this. we need a specific policy, both at national and european level. so that's why we asked you these questions. i'm asking you to do three things. make an assessment of what the capacity of small-scale energy in europe really is and what part of our needs can be met by it. come up with a strategy to make the consumer a prosumer in all the member states of the european union. how can we adapt european legislation so that this small-scale energy, this microgeneration, can be included in the 2030 climate and energy package, for example? of course, it is not the only solution. we are now talking about the buzek report. and since i am otherwise overstaying my time, i will now close by thanking my colleagues for their cooperation. mr buzek, we have made an excellent report. i would also like to thank the other colleagues who have worked with us. i'm looking forward to a lot of energy in the future.

- i'm the reporter. - i am addressing you in dutch and i am doing so very deliberately. it is not because it could not have been done in german or english or whatever, but precisely because we as the european parliament and a compliment is in order. we often need these interpreters desperately and they must be there. but the other way round is that if we do not need them, if they are not called upon, they do not have to be there and we as a parliament can save costs. a european citizen understands perfectly well that interpreters are needed for us to do our work here. but full cabins, unused, he doesn't understand. especially when you realize that costs $1,025 per person per day. and fortunately, steps have been taken in recent years, for example by meetings in brussels, but also here, to spread the week better. and in that respect it is a pity that we are now talking after 11 o'clock at night, because in the interests of cost-efficiency of the interpretation service we should have finished this debate before 11 o'clock at night. but even if there have been costs, parliament must now act decisively. in my report we point out a number of points where we think action is needed. first of all, tackling - and that really takes priority - late cancellations of meetings. in the case of delegation trips, it is as high as 20%! the cost of these late cancellations can be as high as eur 5 million per year. that's a real cost savings, interpreters getting paid while not using the service. secondly, there is a greater awareness of the fact that the use of interpreters is costly. awareness-raising among staff, among civil servants, but also among parliamentarians themselves. the report makes a number of suggestions on how this awareness could be improved. thirdly, the introduction of interpretation on demand. we simply need to make much more use of it, to know much better which languages need interpretation and to offer only those languages. fourthly, better information to the committee on budgetary control to keep a close eye on the cost developments of this expenditure. we also call on the european court of auditors to review the language regime, not only in the european parliament but also in the council, the commission and the other institutions. the last time this happened was in 2005, but it is urgent that we look at this again. these are some of the suggestions made in the report for a new and more far-reaching decision on multilingualism and also on limiting its costs. i would like to say this explicitly, because that is our main expectation, our main demand, which we are putting forward here. i wish you the best of luck in that regard.
the group, and i myself, take the view that freedom of movement is a great asset in the world and that the european union must therefore open itself up to all kinds of other countries that want to do the same. but there are also things that can go wrong. and every now and then, something goes wrong. it is therefore good that the legislation now being proposed includes a so-called suspension clause, which means that if things go wrong, we can take measures to prevent abuse. that is a very important asset for the liberal group. we also think it is important that if a country abolishes certain arrangements with regard to citizens of the european union, we can respond by doing the same with regard to that other country. as regards the delegated acts, another important point: the group interprets the legislation in the sense that the possibility of delegated acts remains, and we think that is very important. in short, we can support the rapporteur's present proposal. we thank him for his work and we hope that the proposal will be supported by a majority in this house.

in writing. - i cannot support the thyssen report on european banking supervision. this european supervision is the first pillar of a banking union. the banking union is clearly problematic. weak banks must be recapitalised before they can join the banking union. the question is whether the crisis countries themselves have the money for this recapitalisation. in short, there is a great danger that banks with insufficient capital will enter the banking union. the pre-banking union approach to bad loans is therefore not guaranteed. the size of the capital requirement is unknown to economists. dijsselbloem also confirmed in brussels last week that we do not know. first, the figures must be put on the table so that we can talk fairly about the distribution of the bill. the banking union is also part of a larger package of building blocks proposed by president van rompuy. it also includes economic union, budgetary union and political union, including a stronger position of the european parliament. the sgp group does not think this is a good idea, because the national parliaments deserve to be at the centre. it is up to the member states to implement the necessary cuts and reforms to restore competitiveness and the economy.

mrs mcavan, first of all, thank you very much for your report. it is estimated that 700 000 people die from smoking every year. it has been mentioned a number of times, but it remains a terribly high figure. the tobacco industry, however, prefers to think in terms of other figures, namely profit figures. i'm not the one. i support all measures which discourage people, especially young people, from smoking. and how many smoking parents do not hope that their child will never start? that is the essence of this proposal. we don't ban smoking, we don't take away anyone's job or their job. this is purely a matter of public health. the huge lobby that has been from the tobacco industry. i've received hundreds of letters. e-mails from tobacco and packaging manufacturers. in the past few weeks, i've even been getting free large packages of shag sent to me by lobbyists. i think that kind of deadly gift crosses a line, and i immediately put a return label on it. no fancy or small packages, no added flavorings, no advertising slogans that entice people to start smoking. we also need to be very careful about substitute products, which are designed to keep smoking as a habit. e-cigarettes may be a potential means of helping people quit smoking, but they must be safe. now, finally, this proposal is going to be voted on and i hope that public health will prevail and not the mass lobby.

thank you, mr president. 700,000 deaths a year from smoking. that is the same as the population of cities like lisbon, seville, or riga. that huge number needs to be reduced and this directive is helping to do that. we must not let our health go up in smoke. i also think it's important that tobacco tastes like tobacco and looks like tobacco. for example, additives to cigarettes containing flavourings or vitamins should be prohibited. they're deceptive. they suggest that smoking is good or less harmful to health, which is of course absolutely not the case. young people seem to be sensitive to those flashy packages or those added flavors. for example, adding a chocolate flavor to tobacco reduces the real pungent taste of tobacco and makes young people more likely to become addicted. we shouldn't do that. that is why we must have stronger warnings on the packaging. in canada, it's been a huge success. so i'm going to advocate that.

unfortunately, the european parliament cannot yet give the council a discharge. the european parliament decides on discharge to the commission and the other institutions which implement the eu budget, including the council of ministers and the european council. regulation 966/2012 provides that the other institutions shall make every effort to act on the observations accompanying the european parliament's discharge decision. for some years now the council has not sufficiently answered questions or requests for information from the european parliament and has therefore not cooperated constructively. this hinders the european parliament in deciding on discharge. finally, i call on the council and the commission to continue to implement the commitment to make the member states' declarations of expenditure. if ministers take adequate responsibility for how eu money is spent in their member state, this will definitely help to ensure sound financial management.
of course, this parliament cannot give the council a discharge. if the council refuses in principle to give information to the discharge authority, we simply cannot grant discharge. i wonder why that is. does the council have something to hide? if the president, the vice-president and the secretary-general of this parliament are accountable to the committee on budgetary control for the use of parliament's money, all of them in public, why can the council not do the same? does he feel too bound by a gentlemen's agreement? we're not talking about each other's budgets? if that is the case, i think that gentlemen's agreement should be thrown out as soon as possible, because every european institution must - as far as i am concerned - be held accountable to the public for the use of the money of the citizens. if the council refuses to do so, it sends a bad signal to the citizens of the member states they represent.
