mr president, it is the right time to open negotiations on a direct investment treaty between the eu and china to substitute the current 26 existing bilateral treaties between china and eu member states. eu-china trade has drastically increased over the past couple of years. however, total eu investments in china are seven times larger than vice-versa, and at the same time european businesses need new capital. that is why we should remove investment barriers and agree on clear rules of the road. china has indicated that the market access chapter could be included in a direct investment agreement, which is a precondition for opening the negotiations. there is a large list of current obstacles for european companies looking to do business in china – whether it is discrimination, complex tariff structures or technical barriers to trade – and this should end. as a member of the wto, china is bound to open its markets, enable a level playing field and allow fair competition. this will eventually also benefit european consumers and businesses, particularly smes. as i mentioned, i support the negotiations but urge for strict criteria for transparency, respect for human rights, environmental laws and labour standards. these should be a crucial part of any agreement. goods that are made in labour camps should fall outside of the scope of a new deal, and the eu should insist that human rights and the rule of law feature more prominently on the political agenda of the annual eu-china summit. if these demands are not met, i am sure that this parliament will not endorse such an agreement.
president, mr de gucht, ladies and gentlemen, the opening of trade negotiations with china is an important step for the european union. this would be the first investment protection agreement of its kind since the treaty of lisbon and the union's powers. this agreement has many benefits for europe. it creates a level playing field between member states and also makes us stronger as an eu. at the same time, it will be easier for chinese investors to develop their activities in europe, and that is something we can also use well in this period. that is an important point. this agreement pursues mutual interests and should therefore also effectively ensure that those mutual interests are safeguarded. but that can only happen if we create a level playing field for european and chinese companies. i therefore also think it is important to include strong social and environmental clauses in the investment agreement and to make them a condition for the signing of this agreement. it cannot be that - and i have seen this with reach - that the chinese government takes over the rules and imposes them only on european investors, while chinese companies are subject to a completely different set of rules. i think that is not possible, mr president, because that would be dumping. we must ensure that the concept of reciprocity is very clearly defined in all aspects of this agreement.
mr president, about a month ago the european chamber of commerce in china published its annual position paper on the people's republic of china. as in previous years, the document does not give a rosy picture of the chinese market. if certain barriers to foreign companies were removed, others were put in their place. the main obstacle is and remains unequal market access. in recent years, european investors have been faced with structural barriers. in most cases, the situation has not improved but rather worsened. i therefore call on the commissioner to make a strong case for the position of european companies in the negotiations. market access is not a one-way street! "blue card" is the answer. mr president, i believe that investment and trade are closely intertwined. if you look at what is happening in shanghai at the moment, where there is a liberalisation, a free trade area, it will be interesting to see whether the investments made there actually lead to better trading conditions and better market access. i don't think you can separate that. i'm not an economist. i did take a little economics in my high school education and i'm a historian, so i'm kind of into it. and you can't just separate the two.

- i'm the reporter. - in the previous debate the name was mentioned several times: it's the euro. that is probably one of the most important things we are going to decide on tomorrow in the preceding debate. what is the main objective of eurosur? we want to create a common framework for the exchange of information and cooperation that is much more effective than what is currently in place. and why are we doing that? we want to avoid illegal immigration wherever possible. we want to prevent crossborder crime from happening. but - and this is the most important point that parliament has made - we want to have better possibilities for saving lives, especially in the mediterranean. the last point is one of the most important points raised by the european parliament. it is in article 1 and article 2 of the regulation, but also in several places in the text, that reference is made to the need to save human lives. we are talking about the external borders of europe. and then we literally think of the borders with the neighbouring countries. as regards external borders, we can also speak of airports in certain countries. and the regulation provides that, if member states so wish, they can also be considered as such and participate in it. there has also been much debate in parliament on the inclusion of fundamental human rights in the eurosur convention: the commission has already taken steps to ensure that the european parliament and the council are able to take part in the negotiations on the future of the union. you can find them in various places in the text and parliament attaches great importance to them. the national coordination centres will be established in each member state of the union and will collect information primarily from the authorities currently responsible for protecting the external borders in that country. we hope that it will lead to improved cooperation in the country itself, because even there the services do not always work together. this information will be passed on to frontex in warsaw and in turn frontex will pass on all useful information to the member states as soon as possible. they should then see if they can use the frontex situation chart to improve the situation. frontex will also be able to play a role in ensuring that certain member states receive assistance or not. the new regulation also allows us to use military information. this military information is not automatically available, but according to the principle of 'what is necessary to know in order to implement it successfully'. experience will show how this will develop in practice. i have already pointed out the importance which parliament attaches to the protection of privacy. it clearly states that information can only be exchanged with other authorities in case of emergency. the european parliament also attaches great importance to the use of existing eu institutions, the european satellite centre, the european fisheries agency, the european maritime safety agency and so on. and above all, better cooperation with neighbouring countries. it has been mentioned a great deal in the previous debate. we leave open the possibility of appointing certain people in the european union embassies to monitor the refugee situation in the country concerned. they can then discuss with the government what can be done about it. there is an opportunity to work with the united kingdom and ireland. in short, i can only recommend this report to the house. i think it's a good report. and i would like to end by thanking my shadow rapporteurs and their staff, who have played a very fine role in bringing about this agreement.
rapporteur. − madam president, first i want to thank everybody for their contributions to this debate and for the kind words that were sometimes spoken. as i said earlier, i can recommend the agreement to this house. that implies automatically that i reject all the amendments, even the amendments from the greens. i do not think that they will improve the text; the saving of human lives is treated in great detail in the text, so an extra amendment does not make it better, and on top of that, if it were adopted, it would jeopardise the whole agreement that we have at the moment and i do not think that is worth it. i think we have to start with eurosur as quickly as possible, so i would strongly advise this house not to vote for the amendments by the green group, despite their very valuable contribution and their active participation. thank you all very much. i hope that after tomorrow we will have a eurosur regulation.

on behalf of the verts/ale group. – mr president, russia is our biggest neighbour and i would very much like russia to get closer to the eu – more trade, more travel, more ties in all respects – but the actions of the putin regime are instead pushing russia further away from us. the anti-gay propaganda law is a horrible symbol of the general crackdown on anyone who has an opinion that does not suit mr putin. after the law dubbing all ngos that get financing from abroad as ‘foreign agents’, this is a further attempt to silence civil rights defenders, with horrific consequences. young gays are threatened and persecuted by vigilantes, violence against lgbt people has increased and activists are detained and prosecuted. meanwhile, the eu and some of the member states are still treating the putin regime as a regular, friendly government. my own country, the netherlands, is this year celebrating 400 years of ties with russia with all kinds of festivities here and there, although the friendship seems to be cooling off rather quickly at the moment. it is time that we stopped treating russia and the russian authorities as friends because they are not. they are not our friends. they are not the friends of civil rights or even of the russian people. they are only the friends of their own power. let us be clear: there can be no further trade liberalisation and no further visa liberalisation without more respect for civil rights and for the rights of lgbt people in russia. there can be no meeting russian counterparts or celebrating the glorious culture and history of russia here without emphasising the need for human rights, such as freedom of expression. there must be consequences for russia in the council of europe as well. above all, let us not look away as the situation gradually gets worse every day. let us do what we can, mostly to support the russian people as their government abandons them.

on behalf of the verts/ale group. – mr president, i would like to thank everybody for the resolution that is now on the table. what i would like to stress is that 2015 is very close. we are just two years away from the moment when a new climate deal should be agreed upon, and this time it should not fail, as it did in 2009 in copenhagen – sorry, mrs hedegaard, that was not meant personally. we cannot afford another failure. i also think that my colleagues on the right have to realise that they should be reading the ipcc reports a bit more carefully. if you look at the cumulative emissions, the ipcc clearly states that, if you want to meet the 2˚ celsius target, cumulative emissions can only reach one trillion tonnes of carbon. we are already over half way there and, if we just follow the baseline, in three decades we will have reached one trillion tonnes of carbon. then we will be passing the 2˚ limit. that is where we are at the moment, and that is also why we need ambitious targets on the table now. usually it is parliament that takes the first step, because we know that in september of next year, ban ki-moon will call all world leaders to come forward with their pledges. so in march, the leaders of the eu will come together and perhaps think of some targets. this is also the moment when parliament should be pushing our own leaders in the eu to come forward with ambitious targets. that is also part of this resolution now, because we need the targets now in order to make sure the eu leaders come forward in march. then later on we, as europe, can play the leading role in the ban ki-moon summit in september – because if we do not do so, who is going to take the first step? rapporteur for the opinion of the committee on civil liberties and internal affairs. - mr president, it is very kind of you to give me the floor after i was late. as for the position of the civil liberties committee on the 2014 budget: we are generally in favour of the economic policy proposed by our rapporteur. the european union is not a party to the maastricht treaty, but it is a party to the treaty of rome. the committee has, however, approved a number of exceptions which have fortunately been adopted by the rapporteur. these exceptions will not be surprising. in view of the events in lampedusa, it is extremely necessary to provide additional funding to frontex, easo and other bodies which have a lot to do with asylum seekers. that is the view of the committee on civil liberties. that is what has been achieved in parliament's budget proposal and i hope that the council will support that in the near future.
i thank the rapporteur because it is good that there is now a comprehensive report on the fight against organised crime, with particular emphasis on financial crime. corruption is one such form of crime which is still on the increase in a number of member states. i am therefore looking forward to the first report from the european commission and i ask commissioner malmström: will the commission finally comply with its own reporting obligations under the un convention? sometimes, however, the report is clear. in particular, the rights of suspects are being ignored. the report is very sloppy in its treatment of the question of the denial of the right to vote. i can imagine that the italians no longer need another resurrection of berlusconi, but it is really up to the judge to prevent this by means of an additional punishment and not to politicians to decide that themselves. the report also concludes very quickly that there should be a european public prosecutor. i do not think the commission's arguments are very convincing in this respect. i have tabled amendments on these matters with my colleagues and, depending on them, i shall vote in favour or abstain.
more than 800 000 victims of human trafficking in europe, mr president. that is about as many people as live in the city of amsterdam. 270 000 of them are victims of sexual exploitation. that's the population of the city of utrecht. this is unacceptable. we have to do something together. some have already referred to that. i am thinking in any case of the following action points: the first. we must follow the swedish model in europe, which means that prostitution must be made a criminal offence; the second. cooperation between national police and judicial bodies and i think we should go back to preactions, to border controls. europol and eurojust should assist in this process; the third. i think it is also necessary for us to suspend the extension of the schengen area for the time being. the countries wishing to join must first demonstrate that they are effectively fighting corruption and organised crime and that they have an independent judiciary. then we can move on. this is necessary, mr president, and i would like to hear from the commissioner.
president. - the next item is the report (a4-0173/97) by mr pimenta, on behalf of the committee on legal affairs and citizens' rights, on the proposal for a council regulation (ec) amending regulation (eec) no 1408/71 on the application of social security schemes to employed persons and their families moving within the community (com (97) 0406 - c4-0173/97). it has been said on more than one occasion that the free movement of persons is mainly the free movement of criminals. in the meantime, each country must arm itself against the biggest rapaille to be found in the eu. in the netherlands we have no answer to the moroccan street terrorism and we are not up to the crime that is happening in the balkans. the insolence of bulgarian gangs to apply for millions of unjustified subsidies in the netherlands. romanians who steal irreplaceable paintings in rotterdam and then turn out to burn them. gangs from eastern europe stealing hundreds of smartphones at major events. how are you supposed to fight all this? mr president, i can tell you that. contrary to what this report says, we do not need more europe, but less. we have learned that open borders do not work. the member states themselves must decide who gets in and who gets out. it's the only way.
i would like to begin by thanking my colleague mr iacolino and, of course, the chairman of the committee, mrs alfano, for the work they have done. i myself have been involved in working visits to eurojust and europol and i think that the committee has done an extraordinary amount of work between all the companies. then the scale of the problem: there are 3,600 criminal organizations in europe that are home to all markets. we see an increasing blending of the underworld and the upper world. there is an investment from the underworld into the upper world, and that makes our economic system extremely vulnerable. what else do we see? the fact that organized crime knows best how the european single market works should be a serious warning to us. that is why i ask mrs malmström: is there anyone else in the european union who sees the coherence between all our activities? we're very active with different agencies, different programs. we have europol, we have the stockholm programme, we have the prüm convention. is there someone coordinating and monitoring all this? what about the implementation problem? you have already mentioned this in your own introduction. yes, you are responsible for that, but do you also have a stake? now, there's all sorts of things happening and i still get the impression that we're not the winning side. unfortunately, the winning party at the moment is still often organized crime. two more elements: the transfer to non-member states. we see increasing activity from ukraine and other countries. and finally , the administrative approach. it is not only the fight against crime that is important, but also the administrative approach.
mafia practices are widespread in europe. human trafficking, illegal organ trafficking, arms trafficking, but also corruption have a devastating effect on our societies. the money made from such practices is being laundered by all sorts of new forms of crime, such as match-fixing. member states are often not or hardly aware of this and are still working together far too little to deal with it adequately. the crim report makes concrete recommendations to the member states for a coherent approach to organised crime in europe. we must strengthen the confidence of our citizens by showing that we are taking organized crime seriously. the same applies to the people who are causing this crisis in the eu, the rogue market traders. in order to strengthen confidence in financial markets, it is important that member states speed up the adoption of the market abuse directive, at the latest by tomorrow.
- a writer. − the appointment of a member of the court of auditors is subject to a procedure laid down in the treaty. the council shall appoint one after obtaining the opinion of parliament. in the past, there have been a few instances where the advice has been negative. nevertheless, the council then wisely decided to appoint that candidate. since then, parliament has asked the court of auditors' nominee in advance what it would do if parliament's opinion were negative. that has happened a few times in the past and a few candidates have said that they would then withdraw. i think that's very honorable. we now have the case of the croatian candidate who stated during the hearing: if parliament finds it unsuitable, i shall withdraw. and then he confirmed that in a letter dated june 5 of this year, in which he said: "during the hearing i said that i would withdraw my nomination if the eu parliament does not support my nomination. i will keep my word". that's strong language, as far as i'm concerned. nevertheless, the council decided to appoint the man. so the big question is: the council was aware that the croatian candidate, mr neven mates, had made this statement to parliament and if so, why did the council decide to appoint the man? because that's a very serious matter. the man has lied to a freely elected parliament and in all our democracies that is a capital sin. i think it is also bad for the reputation of the court of auditors as a whole. how can they have someone in their midst who is lying to this parliament? and why have the members of the court of auditors not protested? i think that in this case there is only one honourable way out for the croatian candidate and that is to withdraw. i think that if someone breaks his word to a parliament, fruitful cooperation in the future is out of the question; if the man were to stay, it would spoil the atmosphere between the whole court and this parliament for years to come. that is a bad thing for europe and i would therefore like to know what the council is going to do about it. does he find the conduct of this candidate understandable, plausible, permissible?
the commission is also proposing to extend the scope of the directive to include the use of the term 'common carrier' to cover the transport of dangerous goods by road. of course you can. there were some incidents, too, so it's good to look into that. the results came out recently, and the conclusion is: these four countries have very little to worry about. you'd think they'd be cheering! because it's nice to hear that your worries are unnecessary, i guess. but that didn't happen at all. because they weren't happy at all, they were angry! they were angry that the survey did not confirm their view that there would be major problems with benefit tourism. the dutch state secretary teeven said, and i quote, "the stupidest thing you can do is deny problems". well, i actually think the stupidest thing you can do is hold on to your own theory when the facts prove that theory wrong. and then you put all your energy into a problem that's negligible, when there are much bigger problems to be dealt with. the free movement of workers, temporary workers, posted workers and selfemployed workers is not without problems. there is underpayment, exploitation and displacement, particularly because the free movement of services allows some of these things legally. the only way to really fight this kind of thing is equal pay for equal work in all structures. countries can already do a lot of things to address the problems themselves, like checking for rights. but in the netherlands, it is the labour inspection that is being cut back. and germany is still refusing to introduce a minimum wage. mr president, free movement must be put on the right track and we do that by tackling real problems and not by spitting out empty rhetoric against all the facts for a few extra votes at the next election.
i am absolutely in favour of free movement, mr president, because it has brought us prosperity and it still brings us prosperity. but the problem we face is that we have to convince our european citizens that all abuse is punished. and that means that there's a lot more and a lot better to control. if the dutch minister for social affairs makes any comments, i would refer them back to him if i were the commissioner and say: check it out! but that does not mean that we should turn a blind eye to the problems. i am not sure that the commissioner has been able to give us a clear answer to this question. does he have health insurance elsewhere in europe? if a tunnel is built in maastricht, where polish and portuguese workers are working, where social security is not paid and where, moreover, far too high fees are charged, then i know that these kinds of problems must be solved. and that is something that the eu and the member states themselves must do.

everywhere, banks that have run into trouble are being recapitalized, bailed out or even declared bankrupt. it is up to each democratically elected government to decide whether its own taxpayers should contribute to the rescue of a national bank, except in the european union. the eu has no message for the democracies of the member states, and the banking union dreamed of by the euro-lovers is another example of this. with a banking union, the esm, to which the netherlands contributes as much as 40 billion euros, risks being used to directly fund distressed banks in europe without the involvement of national governments. mr president, the euro-lovers always have the euphemism: a banking union with european banking supervision. but of course, it comes down to the discharge of debts. all the debts, especially from the south, are thrown into one big pile and then the north can turn around. in other words, those who make a mess of it get away with it. the dutch are now allowed to give away their hard-earned money because of the mismanagement of banks elsewhere in europe. in short, a banking union means a financial disaster for the netherlands and its citizens. madam president, ladies and gentlemen: whether you like it or not, the turning point will be the next elections, in may next year.
rapporteur for the opinion of the committee on women’s rights and gender equality. − mr president, i am a great supporter of the european semester in theory. we desperately need more coordination if we are to emerge from the crisis, and if we are to combat unemployment and poverty. but i find it a bit depressing that, in practice, the semester does not seem to be genuinely delivering, since it is still inherently flawed. it is clear that we are nowhere near achieving the 2020 goals by 2020. far deeper, concerted reform is needed if we are to move towards a sustainable economy with job-rich growth. i would therefore like to ask the commission two questions. firstly, will the commission make environmental taxation and the elimination of environmentally-harmful subsidies a key priority in the annual growth survey? while countries are told to cut down on social expenses, europe is at the same time still massively subsidising environmentally-harmful industries. this is, of course, insane. we should reduce taxation on labour and shift it to environmental taxation. secondly, in this report, parliament will strongly call for a social dimension of the emu, by including social indicators for macro-economic imbalances, among other things. however, it worries me a bit that the commission calls these indicators ‘auxiliary’. so i ask: will the commission ensure that social indicators are put on an equal footing with economic and financial ones in its policy guidance?
president. - the member states have agreed in article 5 of the treaty to coordinate their economic policies with a view to the functioning of the internal market. the eurogroup has agreed not to have excessive deficits, in view of the sustainability of the euro. but what we have not agreed is to coordinate social policy, let alone to take it to the centre, something which the european parliament now wants. that is illegal policy, and it is also unwise policy. decades of socialist policies have already driven the share of government in the total economy to unsustainable heights, and the taxes to pay for those policies squeeze the life out of every economy. it is becoming a habit that both the european commission and the european parliament ignore the treaty. the commission has always had ideas which were not well received in the member states but which brussels is still trying to implement. if this parliament is to call for anything, it is for a small government, small debts, low taxes. that, mr president, is the recipe for restoring economic growth. and then we will have no need at all for the european economic semester!
the economic recovery in europe is slowly gaining momentum. the storm of the crisis has subsided, thanks in part to the new economic governance in which this parliament has also played an important role. but, mr president, the urgency is still high, because the level of unemployment in the member states is unacceptable. so to restore confidence, sufficient action and commitment from the member states is also needed, and they must be supported by their parliaments. the commission's recommendations for structural reforms of the public service budget, of the labour market, are crucial. for our group, the ppe group, a social europe is a europe that creates jobs. and what do we see too often in the member states? it is chosen for the short term, for burdens to be added to bring the budget for the coming year into order. but that does not offer any prospect of solid growth. it's costing jobs instead of creating jobs. commissioner rehn, i would encourage you to look closely at the multiannual plans when you assess the budgets next month. are there sufficient structural measures? we are talking about reducing the debt mountain, because it costs unacceptably high interest rates, and that is expensive tax money, of bringing competitiveness into line. the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. because what we need is a healthy breeding ground for our small and mediumsized enterprises, for our job creation engine. that is going to provide us with the jobs that are needed to help the youth who are now being sidelined to get a job.

on behalf of the alde group. – madam president, i would like to thank gaston franco and commissioner potočnik, and also the irish presidency for the role they have played. i would also like to be honest about the rather uninspiring and also misleading title of the seventh environmental action programme, and particularly the subtitle: ‘living well, within the limits of our planet’, which is far too defensive. i would like to rename this action plan the ‘first european action plan to guarantee well-being’ – because that is what it is about. in tomorrow’s world, with 9-10 billion people and double the current number in the middle-income category, efficiency will determine our competitiveness. this is not about labour efficiency; it is about resource efficiency, energy efficiency and changing our economy from a linear to a circular one. that is what this action plan is about. it is not only about resource efficiency, biodiversity, waste and a low-carbon economy, but also about improving the implementation of eu legislation. the commissioner referred to this as the cornerstone of the action plan, and he also mentioned the eu monitoring and control system, which is to be strengthened. i am very satisfied with the agreement we reached with the council, but it is now up to the commission and the council to live up to that agreement, and that is what always proves to be the most difficult thing. look at what happened last year – the greening of the cap was watered down. the council no longer embraces the biodiversity strategy; and the council, together with many of the commissioners, regards sustainability as an obstacle to growth – although it is in fact a criterion for growth. the good thing about what we are discussing here is that this ‘first european action plan to guarantee well-being’ shows that there is another way.
the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council and parliament on the subject of the budget. the commission is forcing member states to put their books in order and to have a balanced budget as much as possible. it is therefore strange - and this is the liberal group's position - that the member states should force the commission to take the opposite position, so our basic position is: if there is an obligation and bills are coming in, those bills have to be paid. we have discussed this morning the draft amending budget no 6 to pay for a number of things. the european union's own resources are in short supply. it is perhaps also good for the member states to realise that the system of own resources we have now clearly needs to be changed. the events of the last months of this financial year are indicative of this. the liberal group will vote in favour of draft amending budget no 6, provided that the amendment tabled by the socialist group is adopted. but before we can proceed to the adoption of the multiannual financial framework - and then i can only repeat what many others have already said - the liberal group also believes that the 3.9 billion, the draft amending budget no 8, must be adopted. that is a strict condition and the same applies to the other conditions set out in the resolution of july this year. the working party on own resources must be clarified before we vote. what is the composition of the group, who will chair it and all that sort of thing, these are not matters which can be resolved after the vote, we must know before then. the same applies, of course, to the legislative work that still needs to be done. i think that this morning's debate shows - and i have already said this - that we need a new system of own resources and i hope that by the rapid completion of this budget the council will realise that it must cooperate in a new system of own resources.
year after year the commission spent far too much on unnecessary projects. this parliament was all for it and often added to it. it was hard-working taxpayers' money, after all. with buckets of money at a time, the money disappeared into the bottomless pits. however, a number of unpaid bills suddenly appeared at the commission, so many that money was needed for the year 2013. the bill was put to the member states, to the hardworking citizen. the council is rightly angry about all these additional bills. but mr la via does not blame the commission, he blames the council. it has provided far too little money. mr president, this is the world upside down! the one who spends the money demands more and more because he gets too little. truly beyond words! i have no doubt that this house will approve the la via report. the socialists, the christian democrats and the liberals will bow to the commission's demand. the bill goes to the citizen: no less money in the wallet, no more party with the euro-clique. the european union is a great example of a great economic crisis, which is both caused and maintained by the european union.
mr president, international skills for people enrolled in academic and vocational education are becoming increasingly important in order for them to enter into an internationalised job market. the lack of mobility in europe hurts our solutions to youth unemployment. we believe that in erasmus plus the student should be the centre of attention at a time when budgets are being cut and young people are paying a disproportionately high price for the lack of leadership in solving the economic crisis in europe. it is essential that we get the biggest output for our investments. for the alde group, efficiency and the integration of procedures and spending, as well as inclusion, were key points. sadly, there was also a bit of a battle in this house to avoid very long wish lists that would water down the proposed impact and efficiency of erasmus plus. we must now focus on quality for the estimated four million people who will have the opportunity to take part in exchanges, and hopefully that number will even increase, through the loan guarantee. yet with the erasmus programme’s budget now significantly increased but still taking up only a small 1.5% of the european budget, let us keep in mind that there is a lot more that we can and must do for the younger generation of europeans looking for opportunity and political leadership.
the european union's educational programmes are most visible to our young people. i think we can be very proud of that, especially at a time when the european union is sometimes looking for new legitimacy. i think we owe a great debt of gratitude to doris pack for the enormous work she has done as rapporteur on this, albeit complex, task. i am very pleased with the extension of the target groups, particularly to secondary vocational education. we all know that students in science and higher vocational education are very familiar with the erasmus programme. but we are now going to expand to secondary vocational education, to the handicapped students and certainly to sports. and don't say that sport is not a job, because of course there are thousands of young people who find their jobs in sport. i'm also pleased with the relationship with youth unemployment. i sometimes get the impression that the erasmus programme was used too much as a non-binding study programme and had too little direct relation to the labour market. if that could be further tightened in the future, i think we are taking a good step today.
on behalf of the alde group. – mr president, the cultural and creative sectors hardly ever feature prominently on our political agendas. however, they are good for six to seven million jobs in europe and also have an impact on attracting tourists and businesses and fostering innovation – and, more importantly, arts and culture, expression and creativity are essential ingredients for open societies. the intrinsic value of culture cannot always be measured by a price tag. we try, through creative europe, to make a modest contribution by making the eu budget for creative and media programmes more efficient and streamlined, but compared to the amount of the multiannual financial framework – on which we will vote this week – that goes to agricultural subsidies, we fall far short of the future-proof investments that europe needs. having said that, it is important to act even when budgets are under pressure. for the liberal group, the key goal was to be more efficient by simplifying the programmes but parliament had a problem holding that focus, with so many amendments and long wish lists seeking to change the programme. one good new feature is the access to private funding, which could not only help the cultural and creative industries but also increase knowledge within financial institutions about the cultural and creative sectors. a priority we consider to have been missed out is that of reaping the full benefits of digitisation. while including gaming is good, our group would have liked to see better and speedier copyright reform and provision for ensuring that we use new technologies more efficiently so as to cut red tape and make it easier for audiences around the world to access the cultural and creative content of which we are so proud.
president. - mr president, we have today, as it seems, a new multiannual budget. that is a good thing, as far as i am concerned. there's one downside to this: it's for a seven-year period. i believe that a five-year period is better for a multiannual budget and i hope that this will be the case in the future from 2020 onwards. as for the mid-term review. it may be that changes can be made by the new parliament, but i am pessimistic as long as unanimity in the council is still needed to achieve that. the most important thing about this budget is that it is 3.5% lower than the previous budget. i think that is a good sign for the people of europe, now that we are all faced with the need to cut back. we have also reached the point where we will have more flexibility. that seems to me to be a very good thing. the big drawback is that the margins are extremely small. i am particularly concerned about one subject, and that is the outbreak of animal diseases. we've had foot-and-mouth disease and swine fever in the past, and you name it. they could be paid because there were always large margins in the budgets. this is no longer the case and i wonder how the commission will pay if there is a real outbreak of a new disease. then the system of own resources. i think that a system of new own resources should be introduced. this working party is extremely important. the council has made a few clarifications. it is bound to do so, but the council statement says a little too much could instead of should and too much possibly instead of a direct possibility being offered. i would like the council to confirm once again that it really wants to achieve this as soon as possible. i think we need to. as for the rest, we must fill in the names as quickly as possible and i can only hope that the new multiannual budget will live up to expectations, because many have high expectations of it.
the new multiannual financial framework is finally up for vote. i am satisfied with the reduction compared with the previous mff, but i hope that this will also mean that the annual budgets will be reduced. the member states are also having to cut back on their national budgets as a result of the economic crisis. unfortunately, the tone in this parliament is different and many feel that more money is the answer. i would like to see europe focusing more on its core tasks and leaving areas such as social policy, culture and sport to the member states. the planned mid-term review should not yet be used to increase the mff. i find the own resources route unacceptable. this gives the eu undesirable state features. the member states must provide the eu with the necessary financial resources and thus retain control over european policies and the budget, because in the end it is the citizens who have to pay for everything.
mr president, i have heard three things in this debate. it was about the size of the budget. at a time when families, provinces, municipalities and member states are tightening their belts, europe should do the same. second, it was about what we spend our money on. the commission has already taken steps to ensure that the european union is able to meet the challenges of the 21st century, such as access to raw materials, sustainable energy and maintaining our competitiveness. the third point was: how is the european budget financed? and yes, we have another working group on own resources, a déjà vu. but there's one huge elephant in this room that we've all been talking about, and i think that's what it's all about. i have heard a little bit of murmuring and complaining from the far right against the annual conclusions of the court of auditors and then they have left this chamber. there is a solution to the challenge of better accountability of european money - that is the point - and that is national management declarations. that was once a priority of the european parliament, but i no longer hear from anyone about it. it is also a shame that the european commission does not recognise this as a priority, but neither does the council or member states such as the netherlands, which have traditionally considered it a priority. the dutch prime minister, mr rutten - i haven't heard from him. it is precisely in a better accounting of european money that lies the legitimacy of our budget and the policies we finance with it. if we do not work to improve accountability, that legitimacy will suffer. the result will be that the next budget negotiations will only become more acute and will only result in camps being further apart in the trenches. we can't let that happen. so my advice is: i am not saying that the european parliament should not be involved in the budget, but that it should be involved in the budget itself, and that it should be involved in the budget itself.

- i'm the reporter. − at the beginning of this afternoon, after the vote on the multiannual financial framework, it is very pleasing to be able to immediately join the largest package of investments for the coming years, some 325 billion euro, to find a way out of the crisis. that's what this is all about. the reforms, once every seven years, which are now being decided here today, are much more urgent than the adjustments before them. for the first time, the european parliament is also sitting with the council, with the ministers of the member states, as an equal partner in the framework of full co-decision. we have collectively brought about a shift - again - to make these changes, these investments possible. but we've broken more records. the number of trialogue agreements is over 80 for the general regulation alone. that is a european record, as far as i know, and it also shows how dedicated we have been to this. a reformed investment policy across five funds, which we are implementing in the cities and member states close to our citizens, businesses and organisations. the five funds now covered by the general regulation form the central investment frameworks of the european budget. the european cohesion funds, as they have been called so far, are changing from compensatory funds for the transfer of money from rich member states to poor regions into a targeted investment instrument for knowledge, sustainability and jobs. the steering changes too. instead of much emphasis on ex post evaluation, we now work with emphatic ex ante evaluation and commitment. an agreement will be concluded with all the parties concerned, regions, cities and partners in each member state. this contribution to smarter, greener and also more inclusive is at the heart of those debates in advance in the member states. this will prevent too much - i might say too much - being invested in concrete and asphalt and too little in knowledge, training and competitiveness. we are also putting an end to the imbalance in southern europe - over-investment in infrastructure and, as i said, under-investment in competitiveness, knowledge, innovation and employment - which is now causing tens of thousands of well-educated young people to leave each month from countries like spain and portugal alone, from the convergence regions. the new translation of the principle of solidarity in the eu through these structural and investment funds combines in a new way the central knowledge areas in europe with regions, but also all developing regions and cities. the focus through the thematic concentration in the funds and the introduction of smart specialisation is that change, the basis for change. the earlier inclusion in the lisbon treaty of the objective of territorial cohesion provided an important legal basis for this. we are making sure that these cohesion euros are more profitable and are thus also sending a very clear signal. the european social fund's share of cohesion funding is to be increased from 16% to 23.1%. and our instruments are being adjusted - indeed - more towards guaranteeing, loans and revolving funds. i would like to thank in particular - and i would like to conclude - my co-rapporteur, mrs constanze krehl, mrs hübner and all the other rapporteurs on cooperation, and the team. i'll do that again at closing, because it's been teamwork for the last year and a half.

rapporteur for the opinion of the committee on employment and social affairs. − equality between men and women is a fundamental european right. it was already enshrined in the treaty of rome in 1957. the european commission can therefore not only propose legislation to guarantee this right, but must do so. one of the areas where equality between men and women is still far from being achieved, both striking and distressing, is at the top of the corporate ladder. particularly agonizing in these times of crisis. we have learned with great pain that economic decision-making has a huge impact on the lives of all people in the eu. it is simply undemocratic to exclude half the population from this decision-making. so my thanks go to commissioner reding, who has finally come up with a good and nuanced proposal to do something about this. i would also like to thank the rapporteurs and the members of the committee on legal affairs and the committee on women's rights. we have left the proposal essentially intact, but i think we have improved it considerably technically and have also been able to remove many of the objections raised by companies and member states. now it's up to the council. it's a pity he's not represented here. it is good news that in germany a coalition agreement has been made on women in the top echelons of the business community there. then we need one more country. i have placed my hopes in estonia. then we can start negotiating. i hope the council will do everything possible to sit down with us as soon as possible. there is now a wonderful opportunity to take a very important step towards equality for men and women in europe. let's all grab that opportunity with both hands.
blue-card question. – i am quite sure that eventually, at some point in the very far future, taking no legislative action and having no sanctions will lead to more equality in boardrooms, but i am very sure that i will not be alive to see it. how will you, with the measures you are proposing, make sure that you and i will be alive to see 40% female representation in the board rooms of every large company across europe?
they always know how to euphemistically put it around here. we are talking about improving the gender ratio of non-executive directors of listed companies. i prefer to call the beast by its name: women's quotas. that's what it's all about. but improvement is certainly not that. quite the contrary. the commission considers that the fact that some member states take sovereign initiatives to improve the gender ratio and that others do not take any action at all is a distortion of the internal market. it is a pretext for further harmonisation of rules at eu level. the commission is not one to shy away from this. it wants at least 40% of the non-executive board members to be women, and that across the european union. mr president, i say to you, first of all: the court of justice has held that the principle of equal treatment for men and women is not precluded by article 2 of the treaty on european union. secondly: the selection and application process should not be based on gender but on the qualities and competences of the individuals. thirdly: the commission has already made a number of proposals to the council and parliament, and i am sure that the council will be able to accept them. in norway, where a women's quota was introduced in 2003, firms have suffered a decline in value. fourthly: women who really want to get to the top do so on their own.

the rapporteur for the opinion of the committee on budgetary control. - mr president, food is a strategic asset and there are fewer and fewer young farmers in europe who can provide our daily bread. every young farmer i speak to does so with great enthusiasm, but above all he demands that europe give him a fair chance. and then i have three concerns. first of all, young entrepreneurs are ambitious when it comes to sustainable production, but they want to be measured by results. i think the current greening is more like detailed rules - almost hoops that entrepreneurs have to jump through like trained poodles to get to their basic premium - than a real incentive. they want equal treatment on the market and not to import products which do not meet the standards we in europe set for our entrepreneurs. in fact, they do not want us to ban lead-acid batteries in europe first and then allow the european commission to accept as perfectly normal that products which are still produced in this way in europe are being put on the market in a vacuum. and entrepreneurs want clarity and clarity. no european commission trying to convince her through all sorts of back rooms and delegated acts. we will be busy in the coming years adjusting this deal and i hope that in doing so we will listen more to young entrepreneurs and to the common sense of farmers.
in writing. − cyprus is faced with an extreme crisis, and i have abstained on granting additional funds to the country as, despite the fact that i am against the ever-increasing eu budget, eu-driven austerity policies have exacerbated the crisis in countries such as greece, spain and cyprus. thus, there is not only a need for extra funds for the countries in crisis, but there is also an urgent need for a shift in policy.
in writing. - i support the new cap on many points; i am sorry, however, that i had to abstain in the final vote. i cannot agree with the greening requirement that farmers should withdraw 5 per cent of their area from food production. this requirement creates a disproportionate burden. i think that farmers should at least have been given an alternative interpretation which does not require the withdrawal of agricultural land from food production.
i am glad that the framework for innovation policy for next year is ready. it is also the final piece of the innovation union. thank you to teresa riera madurell for leading this and to all those who contributed to it, like marisa matias, and also to other colleagues who have worked hard on this. thank you also for the good work done by the eit. there are important improvements in european policy, such as a focus on societal challenges, a special pillar for businesses and simplification of administrative procedures. but it is important that we are concrete, that the money is well-targeted - not wasteful - and that the bureaucracy is actually reduced. but this is only the beginning of making the european union a true innovation union, and much more is needed. i will give you five priorities, commissioner. make innovation a top priority for europe. the 70 billion is a lot of money, but still far too little compared with the 370 billion that goes to agriculture. if the eu is to truly create growth and jobs, much more resources must be devoted to innovation. the most important thing about innovation is that it actually gets to the market. i still see too little focus on bringing it to market. there's too much focus on research. the real innovation is ultimately entrepreneurship and the market. innovation goes hand in hand with european regulation, because innovation is not just about spending money or programmes. the main thing is to try to use the tools that you have, such as regulations, to contribute to innovation, as has happened, for example, with co2 regulations for cars. but it turns out that this regulation can be more ambitious each time, as for example with car noise levels. implementation is also often lacking. the will is there, but the implementation is not. i would like to make two final points. a more holistic approach is needed, with coordination between national and regional policies. and citizens can innovate too. it is they, as innovators, as creators and ultimately as consumers, who are really driving innovation. we do innovation mainly by setting our priorities and not being too nationalistic and not just looking at the funds, but at the goal. the solution, namely to strengthen competitiveness and create jobs, is and will remain a top priority not only at national level but also at european level.
president, commissioner, it is a great pleasure to be here today. firstly, because the regional investment funds were adopted this afternoon. what strikes me is that this is where knowledge comes first. if you set up horizon here today with your budget, you will also have a very large horizon 2 from the ground up in your hands, provided you promote a good way of working together in the regions and cities. i think that's a very important result. what we have achieved in the whole negotiation - we had to consult the council 80 times because it was not that far along - is a multi-fund approach. we have laid down in the texts exactly how the structural funds and horizon funds can be used for exchanges, for training students and people working in companies. so there's a lot more to horizon than what's out there. i should have had mr hahn with me. two ladies is nice, but the hahn can't be missing in this frame. the commissioner was here yesterday. i 'm just saying , we can take an important step now, but i think we have to integrate a lot more in 2020. there is still a wall between the excellence objectives and the rest. i believe that europe deserves an integrated approach in the new european commission, not one that is turned against each other. i myself have now been working as a shadow rapporteur at the eit and i am very happy that the eit is getting its 2.6 billion relaunch. i am glad that we are not only getting a kick out of working with the regions, but that my proposal for regional innovation centres has been accepted by the council and that we can now go in that direction again with money from the investment funds. europe is not just about centres of excellence or regions of excellence. there are a lot of runners-up and we need to reach people in the new member states. it is important to promote the ladder to excellence in order to increase earnings in europe. that's what we can do together. my compliments to all the colleagues and everyone who worked on it.
madam president, intensified cooperation in terms of defence is indispensable if europe wants to play its role on the global stage. but while we are catching up from a fragmented policy, to reach a more integrated policy in europe, the character of defence and security itself is changing. especially in new fields involving new technologies and lacking matching policies we ought to get it right from the start. many key questions remain unanswered. will the eu develop offensive capacity in the context of cyber defence to avert threats to critical infrastructure and, if we do, how can we avoid a cyber-arms race? meanwhile, technologies that undermine our strategic position and the human rights of people in third countries alike go on being exported unchecked. we cannot credibly talk about cyber security if we do not stop the trade in digital arms. we hear of eu member states joining forces to develop a drone. in what kind of operations is its use foreseen and how do these initiatives relate to european ambitions? who would have to authorise the use of offensive capacities on behalf of the eu? in order to make the cfsp future-proof the difficult questions concerning democratic oversight, command and control must be clarified.

on behalf of the verts/ale group. – mr president, the committee on employment and social affairs started asking for a social dimension to economic governance some time in 2011. the council was then still in a state of panic, trying to make the financial markets happy by assuring them that we would cut and cut and cut again. our committee quickly realised that this would have an enormous social price: that poverty would rise, that unemployment would rise, that inequality would rise. people would feel abandoned by their governments and by us. that is exactly what happened and, in crisis countries especially, the social situation has become unacceptable – families have been evicted, people have been left without healthcare – without food even – and people are angry at europe, at politics and at life. in several reports we have therefore expressed the need for a true social europe, and now, finally, there is something on the table: plans for rating countries not only along economic lines, but also along social lines, and plans to introduce a stabiliser. commissioner, i thank you for this. it is a bit late, but better late than never. now, it is time to make sure that these social considerations have a meaningful impact on economic policies, because up to now member states have been asked on the one hand to cut even more, while, on the other hand, being asked to alleviate poverty and unemployment. the fact that these things might be in contradiction with one another has not really been addressed. we need to address the fact that, in some countries, cutting a little less now might actually make their finances more sustainable for the future. we need to address the fact that in europe we have certain social standards and we do not want to drop below them, because we are europe and we do not let people slide into poverty. we do not let people go without healthcare or without basic provisions. other continents might do that, but we are europe and we do not. my colleagues on the right, who are not here now, have surprised me somewhat. they voted for all the reports, but now, when there is something tangible on the table, they seem to be backing out. do they really want social indicators to be nothing more than measurements – to have no policy impact – so that we can see how horribly poverty and unemployment are rising and we can say ‘oh, look’ but not really do anything about it? do they not want the commission even to explore the possibilities for dampening macroeconomic shocks in the future in order to keep the european economy safe? i call on all of us, because people in europe need us now more than ever. let us take action for a true social europe!
on behalf of the alde group. – mr president, i am afraid we have not attracted huge crowds for this life financial instrument, but that does not mean that it is not extremely important. it is relatively small, but it is one of the most successful financial instruments that the european union has and that is why i am extremely satisfied that we are not only increasing it, but also continuing it. i must say that i was surprised by the attitude of the member states on the life instrument: first of all, on the co-financing rate that they were asking for. they seemed to be willing to shift the responsibility of nature and environment progress totally to the european level and onto the national allocations. the success of life is that it is quality-driven and defining the money before looking at the quality of projects is per definition not quality-driven. i am afraid it was another example of how the member states think of the eu budget and am very glad that both parliament and the commission avoided the destruction of the life instrument. i have two remarks and two questions for the commissioner. first of all, we are celebrating life here, but a few weeks ago commissioner barroso put nature legislation on his refit list, and it was among those areas of what he called ‘small things’. i would ask the commissioner whether nature legislation in the eu is among the small things, or are we talking about one of the most important areas we have? the other question is that the funds are limited and we see huge cuts in budgets for nature conservation at national level as well. according to the commission’s own staff working paper, we are only spending 20% of what we should spend on nature conservation. is the european commission willing to make an analysis of what we are going to spend in the next couple of years on nature conservation and see whether that is sufficient to meet our targets for the biodiversity strategy?
